# Changelog

One line per event — phase exit, default flip, accept-or-reject verdict — with
its `docs/experience/` entry. Newest first.

## 2026-09-08

- **error** — **one pool, two consumers, four fixes: the first three were each wrong on a different axis, and #320's green came from an over-allocation on the wrong one.** The training engine's KV pool must cover the eval arms, which submit into the same engine at a length `cli.py` never looks at — `--eval-max-new-tokens` defaults to **2048** against `--max-new-tokens`'s 32. **The defect is older than #318**, established by negative control rather than inferred: with `--eval-n 8` the same exhaustion happens on `92d31bd` (521 blocks) and after (1033), so `--group` defaulting to 8 — equal to the eval concurrency — is what hid it, and reverting #318 would have left it on the default `--eval-n 100`. **#320 (mine) took the max on the ROW axis and left per-row LENGTH at the rollout's cap**, which still exhausts at `--group 8` (520 blocks, needs 1099) — and its arms passed only because `max(2, 8)` handed the narrow group 4x the rows it used, a surplus on the wrong axis absorbing the shortfall on the right one. **A fourth version that maxed length but kept the widest row count over-allocated** — `--group 16` asks 2280 blocks where the eval needs 1136, and on this path the same memory holds gradients, tape and optimizer state. Correct shape: **price each consumer on its own (rows, length) pair, then max** — `max(rollout_batch × ceil(rollout_ctx/16), min(rollout_batch, _EVAL_CONCURRENCY) × ceil(eval_ctx/16)) + 8`, with `eval_ctx` carrying MMLU's 515-token prompt (the figure the 1024 floor was chosen for; that floor's comment covered the prompt half and missed the completion half). The `min()` is **measured, not assumed**: two mechanisms predicted every already-run arm identically, and the discriminating case `--group 4` at eval cap 1500 (4 rows need 384 and pass, 8 would need 768) says eval concurrency is slot-gated — while **my first attempt at that discriminator sat exactly on the 520-block limit, where both hypotheses predict failure and its output is indistinguishable from a decisive one**. Two negative controls, one broken: reverting `cli.py` wholesale went red on `ImportError`, which proves a test can fail rather than that it detects this defect; mutating only the sizing line gives `all 521 blocks in use`, and the new arm fails on its own assertion (`assert 72 >= 266`). **Why a green suite shipped a pool that dies under the default command:** #320's three arms were chosen to cover both branches of its `max()`, every one passed `--max-new-tokens` explicitly, and **none passed `--eval-max-new-tokens` at all** — so no arm ran (wide group, default eval cap), the pair `tilerl train --rl` uses. Assertions existed, branches executed, parameter combination was not the product's: the third variant of one failure today, and **branch coverage is not configuration coverage**. The new arm leaves the flag at its default and reads the figure from the parser. Diagnosis and controls by `tilerl-0a`, `tilerl-48`, `v100-sm70-fp4-55` and `tilerl-27`. — [errors/2026-09-08-three-fixes-three-wrong-axes.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-three-fixes-three-wrong-axes.md)

- **error** — **the training engine's KV pool was sized from the rollout width while the eval arms submit into the same engine at a fixed 8, so `--group 2` exhausted it and `main` was red with five PRs behind it.** My own change had just replaced three literal 8s with `max(args.group, 1)` — correct, and it fixed a real defect where `--group 16` silently ran as two waves — but the new value is computed from **one of the engine's two consumers**. At `--group 2` the pool is `ceil(1024/16) × 2 + 8 = 136` blocks, +1 for the graph's pad block = the **137 the error reported**, against the **512** an 8-row eval arm needs (`gsm8k_accuracy` / `mmlu_accuracy` / the curve all passed a hardcoded `concurrency=8`, unrelated to `--group`). `--group` defaults to 8, which equals the eval width, so the two consumers agreed everywhere except the one test in the tree that passes a smaller group. **My own `_require_group_fits` guard could not see it:** it compares `usable_slots < group`, and the eval arm neither goes through `group` nor exhausts slots — **the guard and the defect are on two different quantities**, and I had covered the one consumer I thought of rather than asking how many submit into the engine. Fixed with `_EVAL_CONCURRENCY = 8` as a module constant referenced by the three call sites and the pool sized `max(rollout_batch, _EVAL_CONCURRENCY)`; `max_batch` deliberately unchanged, since widening it changes how wide the eval batch runs, and `num_slots` **rejected** — it feeds `usable_slots` and therefore `_require_group_fits`, so raising it to 8 makes that guard vacuous for every group in 2..8, trading a slow path that exists only in a test for a silent correctness guard. **Two sessions independently said the existing red `--group 2` test was sufficient; it is not, and widening it proved so in one run.** A three-arm version failed immediately at `{(2,4): 520, (2,4000): 2072, (8,4): 520}` — `max(8, 8)` and `max(2, 8)` are both 8, so **the group branch of that `max()` never executes below 16**: the bug-reproducing test kills the "size on `rollout_batch`" mutant but passes the "constant 8, no `max()`" mutant, whose pool is 2x too small at `--group 16`. Wide arm is 16 for that reason. **A test that reproduces a bug is not a test of its fix**, and a `max()` exercised on one side is not exercised. — [errors/2026-09-08-one-engine-two-consumers-one-sized.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-one-engine-two-consumers-one-sized.md)

- **verdict** — **P1's task was selected on difficulty, and GRPO consumes hesitation: MATH level 5 measures 91% base against GSM8K's 88%, so it is easier, and the +5 pt gate is unsatisfiable.** Three successive readings of level 5's base — **45.8% → 64.0% → 91.0%** — had two instrument artifacts in them, the second being the *lower bound* of `[64%, 96%]` reported as a point (an interval 32 points wide, **6.4x the effect P1 exists to detect**). At cap 6144 the interval is `[91%, 94%]` while the gate demands base+5 = **96%**: perfecting all 6 known-wrong and all 3 still-capped answers reaches 94, so **no adapter can pass, independent of algorithm**. Fixing the gate would not fix it, because the selection premise is gone: **difficulty is a property of the task, hesitation is a property of this model on this task**, and a hard task the model always fails is worth exactly as much to the optimizer as an easy one it always passes. Base accuracy cannot stand in for tied fraction either — the mapping runs through effective independence, which is **itself a function of base** (higher base, more homogeneous group, fewer effective samples, more ties), so at 0.91 the all-correct ties alone are **≥72.6%** and the base→tied slope varies with base: **a proxy whose mapping slope depends on the proxy does not preserve ordering**, which is why this is not a calibration problem. Replaced by the correct-count distribution over 8 samples per question, which yields base, tied mass and the gradient-bearing 1/8–7/8 subset in one measurement — the target quantity rather than a better proxy. Measurements by `tilerl-0a`. — [errors/2026-09-08-difficulty-is-the-task-hesitation-is-the-model.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-difficulty-is-the-task-hesitation-is-the-model.md)
- **error** — **the JIT that made B=16 look 1.168x worse was bias, not noise, because it was correlated with the treatment — and no subtraction can fix that class.** B=16 compiled **141 kernels (237 s) against B=8's 42 (142 s)** *because* it is B=16: each new decode width recompiles three kernels and the wider arm reaches more widths. So the nuisance term is a function of the variable under test, pointing one way every time — it **shifts the estimate rather than widening it**, which is why the verdict reversed from 1.168x worse to **0.762x better** rather than merely getting noisier, and why the clean B=8 figure is 2.55x below the contaminated one. **Subtraction removes a magnitude, not a correlation**, and compilation interleaves with execution in one wall clock so even an exact deduction leaves the arms measured over different effective work. **The warm-up convention is not a defence either:** which widths a run reaches is runtime data (`backend.py:689` keys the per-M GEMV path on `2 <= M <= _MGEMV`, `_MGEMV=3`, while `:782` pads to `_MX=8` so M=4..8 share one cache entry — the compiling widths are the *last three* rows of a shrinking batch, so a step whose rows finish together skips them). It sufficed here only because these arms have no `stop_token_ids` and the shrink sequence repeats identically; a factory-sampler probe loses that. **Doing nothing, subtracting an estimate, and discarding a head step are one class of unclean, and the middle two look processed** — only a count of zero inside the timed window is independent of where the compiles landed, which is what `len(backend._kernels)` differenced per step now asserts, refusing rather than warning. — [errors/2026-09-08-contamination-correlated-with-the-treatment.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-contamination-correlated-with-the-treatment.md)
- **error** — **card 2 read as an orphan holding 66301 MiB while my own job was `Rl` on it, and the command the alert recommends returns a pid that cannot claim it.** Two defects. **(1) A multi-arm wrapper must re-claim per arm.** I ran both throughput arms as `pod_run.sh tput 2 -- bash -c 'for g in 8 16; ...'`, and the claim binds to the python resolved at launch — arm 1's — so it lapsed when arm 1 exited. `pod_run.sh` **exports `pod_run_claim` for exactly this shape**, its own comment says a four-arm run means three windows where the claim reads stale and the card reads orphan, and `scripts/tp_step_arms.py`'s `claim_card(proc.pid)` already implements it: the mechanism, the warning and a working example all existed, and a bare shell loop has nowhere to hang the helper. **(2) `nvidia-smi --query-compute-apps` inside the container reports HOST-namespace pids** — the four it returned (2882914 / 3543694 / 3579292 / 3560146) exist in no container `/proc`, while the holder was 3304585. And **`card_claim.py:201` already records that measurement** (3796003/3796004 against ranks 842276/842277, dated 2026-09-04) and concludes such matching would refuse every correct claim on the pod — while `:1306` prints the advice to use it. A docstring 1100 lines from the message it contradicts is not a guard. Resolved by reading `/proc/3304585/environ`'s **`CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=2`** plus cmdline, not by matching 66294 MiB against 66301 — a byte figure two similar jobs share is a coincidence that looks like proof. Nothing was killed, which was luck. — [errors/2026-09-08-an-orphan-card-with-a-running-job.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-an-orphan-card-with-a-running-job.md)
- **reject** — **the decode fp4 forward runs Ampere `mma.sync` on Hopper by measured choice, not by the #240 defect, and the plan table does not describe the dispatch.** The decode tick is 29.67 ms against a 6.47 ms bandwidth bound (weights 94.4%, KV 0.5% at 4 kv heads), so 4.59x sits in the kernels, and #240 had just found the fp4 **backward** emitting `mma.sync.aligned.m16n8k16` where a 64-thread consumer made `wgmma` unreachable — 2.765x when fixed. The forward does emit the same instruction (`kernels_linear.py:297`, reached at B=8 through `linear_fp4_mma8`, `backend.py:767`) **and that is a verdict already on record**: CHANGELOG 2026-08-28 has mma8 *replacing* the padded WGMMA decode paths, B=8 aggregate d512 219 → 286.7 tok/s (+31%), with M=1 staying scalar because mma8 is 2.2x slower there. Three constants encode a three-way ordering — scalar at M=1, GEMV at 2–3 (`_MGEMV`), mma8 at 4–8 (`_MX`), wgmma above — so **the memory "small-M GEMV is 2.18x slower than WGMMA" inverts the conclusion if used alone**: GEMV loses to WGMMA, WGMMA loses to mma8, and a verdict's direction is meaningless without the alternative it was measured against. **The reusable finding is the dispatch itself.** `_CUDA_PLAN[("linear_fp4", "decode")]` names `linear_fp4_fp8_decode`, but the mma8 branch sits after the lookup and **returns**, so 2 ≤ M ≤ 8 never reaches it — #240's own entry published "prefill and decode fp4 both dispatch to `linear_fp4_fp8`", true for prefill and false for a decode batch of 8, derived from the table. A comment now marks that the branch precedes the table. Enumerated from `_plan` and `_snap_mma_tile` rather than inferred: the compiled WGMMA M tiles are **16/32/64/128** (`backend.py:48`), so the granularity is **16, not 64**, and B=8 → 16 is **monotone 50% → 100%** rows-used with no valley to cross (9–15 all exceed 50%); the only valley is B=2–3 at 12–19%, below the current batch. The next cliff is **B=17**, which crosses into the prefill bucket where bM jumps to 32 and fill falls to 53% — so B=16 is the sweet spot, and it converges with two independent arguments for a bigger batch (long-tail idle slots, and all-tied groups producing no gradient). At B=8 the tensor-core ceiling is **50% by shape**: half the asm operands are literal zeros (`{xa[k].x, zero, xa[k].y, zero}`). Static read, no wall-clock claim, nothing changed on the hot path. — [wins/2026-09-08-the-decode-fp4-dispatch-enumerated.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-08-the-decode-fp4-dispatch-enumerated.md)

- **verdict** — **B=16 fits the card with 57 GiB spare, so the wgmma sweet spot is reachable — and `--group 16` was silently not producing B=16, a defect that would have fabricated its own refutation.** Measured on card 2 (H20, 95.2 GiB), 27B all 64 layers, gen 1024, prompt 256, the on-policy engine shape: **B=8 peak 25.99 GiB, B=16 peak 30.13 GiB (reserved 37.98, 57.24 free), all 16 rollouts at the full 1024** and 1038 ticks, so no second wave. Capacity was the one term the [per-tick floor table](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-four-rulers-the-wrong-size-for-their-object.md) does not compute, and the training path never measures it — `cli.py` passes `num_blocks`, which is what skips `_fit_blocks`, the only code that reads free memory. **The defect:** `cli.py` built the engine with `num_slots=8, max_batch=8` and a pool sized `*8` — three literals — while `--group` is a settable flag defaulting to 8, so a wider group **queued into later ticks** rather than raising (`Engine.__init__` documents exactly this), two waves of 8 at half the rows per tick — silently the 50% tensor-core fill that B=16 exists to remove. Sized from `max(args.group, 1)` and guarded in `train._require_group_fits`, which **raises**: a defect whose symptom is a wrong conclusion needs an assertion, not a warning. In `grpo_loop` rather than the CLI, because three scripts build their own engines and are the callers most likely to run this experiment; reads `usable_slots`, since that is what a request holds from submit to finish. **Three of my own predictions were wrong and are recorded with the result.** (1) The bracket I sent a peer — 26.65 to 28.76 GiB, the upper bound being "every non-weight term doubles" — **did not contain the answer**: the non-weight term went 2.77 → 6.91 GiB, **2.49x for 2x B**, so linear scaling was never an upper bound. The probe's `base`/`after_build`/`peak` triple then split it: **the pool math was right to 1.05x** (+1.78 predicted, +1.87 measured) and the **entire miss is transient, 1.21 → 3.32 GiB**, which conv state, logits, partials and prefill activations are each three orders too small to explain — **recorded as unlocated** rather than attributed, since a decomposition that accounts for half a delta looks exactly like a correct one. (2) The wall clock said B=16 was **1.168x worse per token**, and **that was the JIT inverting the sign**: 183 TileLang compiles totalling 379 s, **141 of them (237 s) in the B=16 arm against 42 (142 s) at B=8** because each new decode width recompiles three kernels — 47% of that arm's 507.9 s, and compile and execution interleave in one clock so it cannot be subtracted. **Re-measured with the gate, `warm_compiles: 0` in both arms: B=16 is 129.93 s/step against 85.27 (1.524x for 2x the tokens) and 7.930 ms/token against 10.409 — 23.8% cheaper, a net win.** The decode half carries it at **0.618x per token, landing between the two available reference points rather than achieving a fraction of either** — the pure fill argument gives 0.500x (assuming per-call time does not change with M, which it does: fp4 0.080 → 0.126 ms) and 48's byte-weighted per-call timings give 0.710x (one ~70 MB shape per dtype, while weights run to 1272 MB and larger ones amortize better). Both are point estimates dressed as limits and they err in opposite directions; a measurement inside the interval says both effects are real. An earlier draft called 0.618x "76% of the 0.500x ideal" — **that denominator does not exist**. Meanwhile `train/token` is 0.994x because `--micro 1` runs one row per backward and cannot benefit. This composes with rather than contradicts 48's falling per-call rate past M=8 (fp4 832.8 → 531.7 GB/s): batch amortization beat it, and a per-call rate is not a per-token cost. **And the two instruments constrain each other to 15%**: 48's per-call timings converted to per-token and weighted by the two quantization paths' bytes give **0.710x** against this **0.618x** — compatible rather than agreeing (one shape per dtype extrapolated to a model is the reconstruction 48 refused to publish, rates moving 39% across shapes), but a whole-step wall clock and a single-kernel timing share no code and no assumption, so this is two readings of different quantities constraining each other rather than one chain walked twice — and the mix was recomputed here from 48's published table rather than quoted, since quoting it would put both numbers on one side and reduce the pair to a restatement. **What the figure cannot decide, recorded beside it** (`tilerl-0a`): the tensor core does not care which 16 rows it has, so 0.762x holds identically for 2 prompts × 8 completions and for 1 × 16 — the figure is **indistinguishable along that axis** and therefore does not constrain the choice, which is decided on tie behaviour instead (an order of magnitude apart between the two). The step time also must not be multiplied by an accuracy: these arms prompt with random token ids, so the reward is noise by construction. (3) My probe's `pool_used` column reads **0 in both arms** — read after the drain, when the blocks are already released; fixed by sampling every 64 ticks mid-drain. **The guard narrowed 12 existing `grpo_loop` callers**, so they were run rather than reasoned about: one failed (a `SimpleNamespace` stub with no `usable_slots`, fixed in the stub — a `getattr` default in the guard would make it pass for everyone, the trap `_require_on_policy` documents) and one was silently weakened (`test_ledger.py` asserted `slots == 8`, a literal that was only right while the CLI's was too; now `slots == 2 == max_batch` under `--group 2`, so it moves with the flag). 13 passed on metal. — [wins/2026-09-08-b16-fits-and-three-predictions-were-wrong.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-08-b16-fits-and-three-predictions-were-wrong.md)
- **verdict** — **the full-vocabulary sort in sampling is REJECTED: it is real waste but 4.0% of a rollout tick on the card, and the 27 s it was built to explain was the forward draining into the first host read.** `reference.top_p_probs` sorts all 248320 logits for a 43-token nucleus, which on CPU at B=8 is 20.53 ms and accounted for the measured 26.6 ms/tick almost exactly. On H20 `tilerl-48` measured the same sort at **0.274 ms** and the per-row loop at 0.790 ms — **1.065 ms of 26.6**, ceiling 0.15 ms/tick. **Four apparatus errors of one shape produced the wrong pricing, and each is worth more than the rejected change.** (1) My fixture drew logits from a random normal, putting the nucleus at **162301 of 248320** — the waste looked like 1.5x, i.e. no finding; peaked logits give 43, a 5776x waste. A distribution can sit at the **worst value on exactly the axis under test**. (2) The CPU ratio did not transfer, 12.4x → 2.26x, and the bound was computable in advance: 7.95 MB of logits through a radix sort is ~0.16 GB of traffic, **~40 µs at 4 TB/s**, so my 20.53 ms is **517x above the target's floor** — an O(V) op whose V is a few MB is compute-bound on a CPU and bandwidth-bound on a GPU. (3) I excluded `.tolist()` with a ruler in the wrong units: 13 ms/sync is three orders above a real D2H, but on an async stream `.tolist()` costs a **drain**, not a transfer, and the decode path holds no synchronize — so the time was **exposed by** it, not in it, which is the correct answer I excluded. (4) My own weight-floor derivation reaches **0.73x** of the measured 24.93 GB footprint (18.23 GB, 6.7 GB unaccounted), and my first pass omitted fp4 block scales entirely, which would have given 0.93x — "close but not violating" — and waved through a real instrument defect. **The utilization conclusion was then checked rather than assumed and does not flip:** the complete per-tick floor at B=8 is **23.337 GB** of which weights are **93.8%** — the weight row **read** from 48's per-key dump, every other row **derived**, each labelled, because 3.14 GB and 3.746 GB share a unit, a magnitude and a decimal count and nothing in the digits says which is a dump. **5.83 ms at 4.00 TB/s, 6.97 at 3.35 — 1.07x the weights-only figure, utilization 20.4%, headroom 4.91x**; the KV term is 0.6% because GQA gives 4 kv heads, not 24. That dump also **locates my 6.7 GB gap and it was a whole tier I did not know existed**: I assumed 0.5 B/param throughout, while measured is 7.499 GB of fp4 **plus 10.625 GB of fp8 at 1 B/param**, a row my table has no line for — 159% of the gap on its own. Two figures refused rather than folded in: 48's proposal to substitute the 21.896 GB weight row for the 23.337 total (it is an addend, and the substitution asserts a decode tick streams no KV), and the reading that dividing by 48's measured **1144.7 GB/s at M=8** shows two thirds of the forward is weight streaming — that rate already contains whatever makes M=8 inefficient, so the ratio is near an identity; nominal answers how much hardware is left, achieved answers what it fails to explain, and they never combine. Same table prices `tilerl-0a`'s B=16 dispatch finding: only per-batch terms double, **+6.2% bytes for 2x the tokens, 1.88x cheaper per token**, so B=16 costs pool capacity and not bandwidth. And the method lesson from 48's side: **three successive per-op tables each summed to the measured step while the attribution was wrong** — a decomposition is built to sum, so the verdict must come from outside the partition. — [errors/2026-09-08-four-rulers-the-wrong-size-for-their-object.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-four-rulers-the-wrong-size-for-their-object.md)
- **reject** — **level 5 is not the harder task P1 selected it for, and the +5 pt criterion is unsatisfiable at the cap that measures it.** The base is **91.0%**, not the 64.0% first reported. At `eval_max_new_tokens=2048` the reading was 64/100, and **32 completions hit that cap with 0 of the 32 correct** while 64 of the 68 that terminated were; nothing exists in the 159 tokens between the longest natural completion (1889) and the cap, so those 32 are one truncated population rather than a distribution's tail. Rerunning **exactly those 32** at 6144 gives **27 correct, 2 wrong, 3 still at the new cap** — so 6 of 100 are known wrong, 3 unknown, and the interval collapses from **[64%, 96%] to [91%, 94%]**. **A cap that scores truncation as wrong yields a lower bound, never the value**, and the evidence for that was in the first reading: the empty 1889–2048 gap proved truncation, and truncated means unscored, which means unknown. **Two consequences.** P1 wants base+5 = **96%, which is 2 pt above the 94% this cap can produce** — an adapter that fixed all 6 known-wrong answers and got all 3 unknowns right scores 94%, so **no `after` value passes**; unsatisfiable, not tight, and the third criterion today to go unchecked against its feasible region. And level 5 was chosen for being *harder* than GSM8K, which failed P1 by being solved at 88.0% with 73 of 81 steps tied at the ceiling — **at 91.0% it is easier, and the 24-pt difficulty gap was the cap**. Raising the cap does not rescue the choice: it costs **2.40x mean generation** (1386 → 3331 tokens) on the denominator of the throughput target *and* makes the task easier. A tied-group fraction read under 2048 measures the cap, since 32% constant all-wrong inflates it by an unattributable amount. The 45.8% (11/24) hand sample the recipe cites is **45 pt low**. Also fixed here: `--json` discarded every progress line, and `test_the_fp8_kv_pool_generates_what_the_bf16_pool_does` guarded on `hasattr(torch, "float8_e4m3fn")` — a property of the torch **build**, true on every target — while allocation is a property of the **device**, so it had always failed on `metal` and always passed on `cpu`, and CI runs cpu only. — [errors/2026-09-08-a-cap-reported-as-a-base.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-a-cap-reported-as-a-base.md)

- **fix, and a withdrawn diagnosis** — **a 43-minute eval with a zero-byte log was not buffering: `--json` discards every progress line before it reaches stdout.** `cli.py:559` is `log = (lambda *a, **k: None) if args.json else print`, and the manifest prints once at the end (`:1059`) — so an eval-only run launched with `--json` has **no progress output to buffer**. Two sessions began treating the healthy run as dead, and the eval arm writing `eval-before.jsonl` only on completion plus a run directory holding just the pre-written manifest means the log is the only signal there is. **My measurement said otherwise and it was confounded**: I launched a second eval with `-u`, read **2720 bytes in 30 s** against 0 bytes at 43 min, and called it one-flag-apart. All 20 of those lines were **TileLang's own compile logger**, and the relaunched twin — with `-u` set — sat at **0 bytes at 8:13** once the kernel cache was warm. The arms differed in the flag *and* in whether anything had to compile, and what I read as the flag's effect was entirely the compiler's. `setsid env PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1` still lands, because a pod job that *does* print and is not run with `--json` would have it held in a 4–8 KB block and per-caller `-u` is how this gap persists; selftest **arm 7** gates it through a `bash -c` wrapper (the case `-u` cannot reach) by reading `sys.stdout.write_through` — the effect, not the variable — with the negative control printing `UNBUF None WT False`. **It does not make a `--json` run observable**, so the second half lands here too: `_progress(as_json)` routes progress to **stderr** under `--json` and keeps stdout one parseable object, replacing the no-op at both call sites. stderr because a caller finds the manifest by first brace and stdout already carries TileLang's C++ cache warnings — it was never a lone object, and keeping our lines off it is what leaves that parse workable. The test asserts both halves, since either alone passes with the defect facing the other way. Two of my own defects were caught by gates that already existed: backticks inside a comment in an **unquoted** heredoc ran as command substitution on the caller (arm 0 caught it — the comment warning about backticks contained them), and a hardcoded `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0` in a job launched against card 6 was refused and killed by the claim check, which would otherwise have been an orphan on card 0 contending with the run it was hedging. Also settled: `/proc/<pid>/fd` cannot say which card a job uses — all eight `nvidia*` nodes are open in every process from the container's device mount — and `nvidia-smi --query-compute-apps` reports host-namespace pids. — [errors/2026-09-08-a-silence-with-no-writer.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-a-silence-with-no-writer.md)

- **default flip** — **P1's +5 pt GSM8K criterion is judged by a test that cannot resolve +5 pt, and reward — the quantity GRPO optimizes — could make P1 read `pass`.** Two halves of the gate the units fix left standing. **(1) The test.** `roadmap.md:57` quotes `SE ~ 2 pt`, which is neither reading: at `eval_n=500` the **unpaired** SE is 3.04–3.16 pt and the 80%-power one-sided **MDE is 7.70 pt against a +5 pt target**, so a real +5 pt improvement fails to register about half the time — and an exit gate's expensive failure is a **miss**, not a false pass. The eval was already paired and the record already held what a paired test needs: both arms score one `eval_rows` list at temperature 0, and `_write_eval_rows` writes one row per problem, its own docstring noting "P1 fell back to the unpaired interval because only totals were kept". Now `_mcnemar` (`cli.py:376`) reports `b`, `c`, `delta`, `se = sqrt(b+c)/n`, `z` — **1.00 pt at 5% discordant, 1.41 at 10%, MDE 1.96–2.77 pt** — recorded as `metrics["gsm8k_paired"]` **beside** the threshold, never replacing it: the threshold is the roadmap's criterion, the paired test says whether the observed move is resolvable. `b+c == 0` returns `se`/`z` of `None` rather than a failure, and unpairable input returns `None`, because conflating those would let a broken pairing read as perfect agreement. **(2) Verdict vs validity.** `gates_pass` was `all(...)` over a flat list, so a validity gate **passing** contributed to "P1 passed". Each gate now carries `kind`: `gsm8k_improves`/`mmlu_holds` are **verdict**, `groups_untied`/`reward_rises`/`ce_falls`/`rollouts_within_cap` are **validity**. This is what `reward_rises` needed instead of the threshold the units entry left open — reward rising **is** the optimizer working, not evidence that RL moved a downstream number, and rising reward with a falling eval is what reward hacking looks like; the roadmap already draws the line with "the task is too easy for this model and **the run says nothing**". Saying nothing is not failing. `gates_pass` stays **unchanged** as the process exit code, since an uninterpretable run is not a success either; `verdict_of(m, kind)` returns `bool | None`, the `None` being a run that has not *tested* P1, and `format_run` annotates only the disagreeing case. **(3)** `eval_{tag}_secs` on both cache paths finally prices `wins/2026-09-05-before-eval-cache.md`'s `pending-remote` — 55 lines of mechanism plus 129 of test, worth it at 15 min per hit and not at 40 s. **The first version cached the duration and the existing test caught it**: the payload is `{k: v for k, v in metrics.items() if "_before" in k}` and `eval_before_secs` matches that filter, so a hit replayed the miss's **0.7447 s** where it had spent **0.0013 s** — the one number the change exists to produce would have been the only wrong one, and would have read as the cache saving nothing. Six mutants, `__pycache__` cleared between each; one survived the first round (`verdict_of`'s `None` collapse, since `all([])` is True) and died once a `steps == 0` case was added — an *absent* metric is not a skip, it vacuous-passes, so the skip must come from the run's shape. 498 passed. — [errors/2026-09-08-a-gate-too-strict-to-be-met.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-a-gate-too-strict-to-be-met.md)
- **fix** — **`scripts/math_jsonl.py` asked for a dataset config that does not exist, so it had never produced a file, and the `level` column added to it three days ago was never exercised.** `EleutherAI/hendrycks_math` has **seven** configs, one per subject, and no `all` — the repo's file list is `algebra/test-*.parquet` and six siblings, no `all/` directory — so `load_dataset(repo, "all")` raises `BuilderConfig 'all' not found` before reading a row. Found while preparing the level-5-only eval that [errors/2026-09-05](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-05-the-eval-file-was-not-the-level-it-was-named.md) named as the follow-up. **That entry's fix landed on a script that could not run**: the `level` column, the written-levels histogram and the boxing-instruction test all describe a path no data had passed through — which is also why the mislabeled file existed, since run 2's four JSONLs came from the throwaway builder that entry deleted, and only one of the two builders was ever fixed. Nothing caught it because `datasets` is **not a declared dependency** (absent from `pyproject.toml`, so absent from `.venv`), and the existing test reads the script as **text** — `assert '+ _INSTRUCTION' in text` — which is the right shape for a script needing network and cannot see whether the line above it executes. Fixed by concatenating the configs **enumerated from the repo** rather than hardcoded, so a subject added upstream is included instead of dropped. Verified against source: test **5000 rows** (L1 437, L2 894, L3 1131, L4 1214, **L5 1324**), train **7500** (564 / 1348 / 1592 / 1690 / **2304**, plus 2 rows labelled `Level ?` that the filter excludes) — the **2304 matches the recipe's claim** for `math_tr_5.jsonl` exactly, confirming that file a second way, and no level-5 row in either split lacks a `\boxed{}`. The level-5 eval file is checked **against the source, not against its own column**: all 500 rows join by problem text, the source's `level` reads `Level 5` for all 500, 500 distinct problems, all 500 carrying the instruction — the check the 09-05 defect lacked. Two mutants, both red: back to `"all"` (`BuilderConfig 'all' not found`) and reading only the first config (`['4'] != ['4', '9']`, a file of the right shape with a sixth of the data). The new test stubs `datasets` with the **real one's failure mode** — an unknown config raises — because a stub that cannot reject does not test a call that can be rejected. **And it is verified on a real dataset in two environments**: this Mac (`datasets` 4.5.0) and the pod (5.0.0, via `HF_ENDPOINT=https://hf-mirror.com`) generate **byte-identical** files, `e5261691418bbf7d`, 500 rows and `{'Level 5': 500}` each — which matters more than usual when the defect was a fix that had never executed. **The 09-05 entry's premise is also false in both clauses**: it says "the pod has neither `datasets` nor network", and the pod has 5.0.0 and reaches the hub through the mirror (only `huggingface.co` direct fails, `Errno 99`). That premise is upstream of the defect it documents — false premise → build on the Mac with a throwaway script → no `--level` filter → mislabeled eval file. `pod_sync.sh`'s true "GitHub is unreachable" is the likely source of the generalisation; the scope of a negative result cannot be widened past the one thing it was observed on. — [errors/2026-09-08-a-generator-that-never-ran.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-a-generator-that-never-ran.md)

- **default flip** — **P1's exit gate was encoded in the wrong units, so a +0.2 pt GSM8K move passed the roadmap's +5 pt criterion.** `roadmap.md:57` states `after - before >= +5 pt (SE ~ 2 pt)`; `_finish` compared `gsm8k_after > gsm8k_before`, and those keys are **counts** (`cli.py:588`, pinned `int` by `test_ledger.py:71,102`) while `mmlu_{tag}` thirteen lines earlier is a **fraction** (`:575`). So passing needed **+1 correct answer of 500**, and a zero-threshold gate on a symmetric null passes about half the time — the recorded P1 verdict could be produced by noise. `mmlu_holds` allowed `-0.03` against the roadmap's `-2 pt`. **The obvious fix is a no-op**: on integers `>= 0.05` is `>= 1` is `> 0`, so `after - before >= 0.05` would have changed the source, passed CI, and altered nothing — the units mismatch, not the looseness, is the root cause, because it made that fix invisible. Now `after >= before + 0.05 * total` with `total` off the manifest (`gsm8k_after_total`), so the bar tracks `--eval-n` instead of hardcoding 25; `gsm8k_{tag}` deliberately stays a count, since every manifest already written holds one. Each threshold reverted **separately** under mutation and each kills the test on its own. **Third defect in the same block, also fixed**: `ce_falls` never writes `ce_first` on the RL path (`cli.py:513-515`, `:679-690`), so the vacuous-pass rule made it report `passed` over nothing on every RL run — not loose, **unconditionally true**. Now marked `skipped` there, so the manifest records "not measured", which is the fact; the gate stays live under SFT and the test asserts a rising CE still fails. **`reward_rises` is left open deliberately** — a bare `>` with no threshold, but `roadmap.md:57-58` has no reward term, so it needs a ruling on whether a diagnostic belongs in a verdict, not a number invented here. **And the first mutation run was void**: an equal-length edit in the same second is invisible to `.pyc` invalidation, so the interpreter reran the old bytecode — caught by comparing `_finish.__code__.co_consts` against the source. Redone with `__pycache__` cleared between mutants, which exposed a surviving one: no case reached the `_total` fallback, where a missing total yields a `None` threshold and a vacuous pass on the gate that matters most. — [errors/2026-09-08-a-threshold-in-the-wrong-units.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-a-threshold-in-the-wrong-units.md)

- **phase exit** — **the run ledger can now support the comparison it is used for: `manifest["engine"]` records the engine config bundle, and an opt-in `eval_curve` records `(step, score, cumulative_secs)`.** Neither existed, and `time_to_score = steps_to_score × seconds_per_step` needs both. The engine gap was found by needing it: six card sessions measured per-forward device time moving **2.16x** with engine configuration, and both pool sizes in that arm were recoverable **only because the probe logged its own flags** — the manifest recorded none of `num_blocks` / `max_total_tokens` / `num_slots` / `decode_graph`. It goes beside `metrics`, **not in `inputs`**: `id = run_id(inputs)` (`ledger.py:41`), so a pool there makes every pool change a different run — **comparability should not change the id**. Values are read off the built engine, since `max_blocks` clamps and the graph reserves a pad row in both pools. The curve closes the other factor: `gsm8k_before`/`after` answer "did it cross a threshold" and **cannot** answer at which step, which is `steps_to_score` itself, and no choice of threshold at the reading end recovers it. Fixed held-out subset, scored at the yield where `invalidate_weights()` has already run, `secs` summed rather than `median × step` because step length is not constant within a run. Both tests carry a second assertion and both caught a live defect: the engine test's context-tracking half caught two arms reporting the same 520 blocks (`ctx` has a 1024 floor), and the curve's monotone half caught cumulative seconds going **down** — 0.143 s at step 4 against 0.148 at step 2, because my accumulator was named `elapsed` and the timings loop rebinds it. And a reading end, because a record nothing reads is not a measurement: `tilerl ledger --time-to-score SCORE` returns the point that first crossed it **plus the interval it was crossed in** (`step 30, in (20, 30]`), never an interpolated step — the target falls between two scoring points and a fitted headline metric is what the curve exists to prevent; three distinct answers (crossed / instrumented-but-never / no curve), since "not instrumented" is not "fell short". **The subset is a different quantity from the gate**, caught by `tilerl-0a` on review: the curve's `score` is over the training run's curve subset while P1's gate reads `gsm8k_after` over 500, and at n=20 the binomial SE is **11.18 pt — 2.2x P1's own +5 pt target**, so the crossing *step* is sampling-limited. `n` and `se_pt` now travel with every answer and the reader warns only when `se_pt >= 5.0`: silent at 500 rows (2.24 pt), loud at 20, because a note on every line reads as boilerplate. **The first point carries the JIT and is flagged**, also from `tilerl-0a`, who ran the curve on `06b16ea`: **2.801 s at step 2 against 0.500 s at step 4 with identical `n`**, 5.6x of TileLang compile that every later point hits warm — so the subset size is calibrated off point two, and point one, the only point a 2-step run produces, is exactly the unrepresentative one. 501 passed. — [wins/2026-09-08-the-ledger-recorded-config-not-worth.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-08-the-ledger-recorded-config-not-worth.md)
- **default flip** — **the GRPO reward now subtracts a length term (`--length-penalty`, default 0.1), so an all-right group orders by length instead of tying at zero advantage.** Run 2's collapse at step 41 of 100 was a correctness-only reward being indifferent between two right answers of any two lengths: nothing preferred the shorter one, the policy lengthened until it truncated at the cap, and 19 of 45 steps produced no gradient (42.2%, 1.08 h of 2.71 h). The term is in the RL reward closure (`cli.py` `_length_aware`) and **not** in `MATCHERS`, because that matcher's count becomes `manifest["metrics"]["gsm8k_*"]` — the number P1's exit criterion reads — so a length term there would sit inside the north-star gate. **λ is not a dial where the defect mostly lives**: in an all-right group it cancels exactly (`adv = −(L−L̄)/std(L)`, no λ), verified through the real `group_advantages` at λ ∈ {1e-4 … 1.0} with `max|Δ| = 1.6e-12` against the closed form; in mixed groups it does matter (`max|Δ|` 9.1e-02 to 7.6e-01) and is bounded by the ordering it must preserve, `λ < cap/(cap−1)` = 2048/2047 = 1.000488520, with the inversion measured between λ=1.0004885 and 1.001. So λ ∈ (0, 1.0] is safe throughout and a tie-fraction sweep would measure a constant on 14 of the 19 target steps. The synthetic reward is left alone because it is a **rate** — its expectation does not grow with length, so the defect is absent there by construction. **Ceiling stated up front: 17 of the 19 steps, not 19** — steps 41 and 44 sat exactly at the cap, where any function of length is constant within the group, and only a bigger cap reaches them. **`pending-remote`: that the policy stops lengthening is unproven and CPU cannot prove it** — it needs one real run with `per_rollout`'s 8 pairs per step and `_within_group_r` showing a shrinking negative within-group correlation. Two mutants gate it, the second being the rejected design: dropping the term goes red, and moving it into `MATCHERS` goes red at λ=0. 24 passed in `tests/test_rl.py`. — [wins/2026-09-08-a-length-term-in-the-grpo-reward.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-08-a-length-term-in-the-grpo-reward.md)
- **reject** — **the tree-wide dead-code audit removes 37 net lines from 65,768, and three of its four lanes remove nothing.** The brief assumed a 200-file `scripts/` tree and a kernel layer full of one-off probes were the obvious cleanup targets. Enumerated rather than searched, they are not. **`scripts/`: 0 deletable** of 202 files and 29,264 lines (45% of the tree). Six entry-point sets were built and each file classified by set membership, so a file is unreached only when it is absent from all six; the 34 UNREACHED files were then opened by hand and every one is load-bearing. **PROVENANCE (84 files, 10,749 lines) is larger than LIVE (82, 14,039)** — `scripts/` is the repository's biggest store of documented evidence, and deleting a file destroys the provenance of a number still quoted in the tree, sometimes with the link semantic and unnamed (`probe_norm_cast_once.py` is behind a ratio its entry states without naming the script). **Kernels: 0 unreachable** across four independent enumerations — 111 module-level functions, 59 registry names, 44 backend dispatch keys, 49 `reference.py` functions. **`src/tilerl/`: 44 deletions against 7 replacement lines, net −37 of 11,754 (0.31%)**, six sites each carrying its own caller-set enumeration; `ruff --select F401,F811,F841` is clean over the whole lane, no legacy/deprecated marker exists in it, and **the no-half-states gate has held** — all four dual-path shapes that looked like stalled migrations resolve to two live paths with a documented selector. **Tests moved the other way**, since a test tree's dead code is a check that cannot fail: one vacuous assert made exact, 7 of 16 tolerance bands sitting over a measured-zero effect tightened, and **86 `__main__` asserts across 10 modules that CI had never once invoked** now run under a `_MIN_MODULES >= 10` ratchet. **The method finding outweighs the diff: five instrument failures in one night, every one permissive, every one pointing at deletion.** A keyword search proves only what someone thought to search for; a name is not a link (a factory `return`, a CI glob, a filesystem round-trip, a name passed as an argument); a `__main__` block is not the file's tail, which inflated a systemic count 2.0x to 872 lines against an AST-measured 436; a two-dot diff moves the moment `main` advances, which is the only moment the check is useful; and a shared checkout 363 commits behind reports a live file **absent**, which reads as ground truth rather than as a fact about one directory. An audit's errors are not randomly signed — they run toward the finding — so enumerate what must be empty and ask whether the instrument's failure mode is the one that agrees with you. — [wins/2026-09-08-the-scripts-tree-needs-nothing.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-08-the-scripts-tree-needs-nothing.md), [wins/2026-09-08-no-dead-kernels-and-a-name-is-not-a-link.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-08-no-dead-kernels-and-a-name-is-not-a-link.md)

- **reject** — **two "missing demote branch" defects withdrawn: a branch that cannot satisfy its own loop is not a fix that was forgotten.** `_demote_one` is a **byte-axis** tool — it clears `entry.state` and leaves the entry in `_by_id`, touching no block. Two loops were filed against for lacking it, and neither has a byte term: `insert`'s count term (`kv_cache.py:1225`) and `evict_until_free`'s block term (`:1330`). In both, a demote branch would write to the tier, re-test an unchanged condition, and evict anyway — one wasted tier write plus a `forget` per entry. Measured on the real store rather than read off the code, with the **boundary row** the original refutation lacked: at `capacity=9` with 9 entries and `state_bytes=2500`, `len == capacity` satisfies the guard's `<=` at the exact count where "can never be true" is load-bearing, and **6 demotions with 0 evictions** come out; count-only pressure gives 0 demotions and 4 evictions, which is correct. On the block axis a forced `_demote_one` returned True and moved `free_blocks` **58 → 58, delta 0**. The second claim was mine and was written *while reviewing and confirming the refutation of the first* — three paragraphs above the sentence in the entry I was citing that already answered it. What survives is a **sizing** defect on neither axis: the tier converts byte pressure into block pressure (peak occupancy **45.6% → 99.1%** purely by enabling it), the block path evicts demoted entries, `_drop` calls `_dram.forget`, and the host copies are orphaned — 103 demotions, 1864 ms, 0 promotions. — [errors/2026-09-08-a-missing-branch-that-cannot-satisfy-its-loop.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-a-missing-branch-that-cannot-satisfy-its-loop.md)

- **reject** — **the one-process two-engine arm for the 2.6x rollout gap is abandoned; the defect is where it was, ~100% unattributed.** Six card sessions. The arm made config the only variable and thereby introduced one no config field can express: **`serve` built second is 2.162x slower than built first, `train` is position-invariant at 1.006x** — which is the whole of the 2.157–2.180x that two arms reported as a finding. A two-engine-in-one-process arm carries a confound the recorded three-process measurement does not have, so a positive result would have been uninterpretable and the negative results were the confound. Surviving: the **residual is refuted** as the location (0.009–0.010 ms/tok, 0.1–0.3% of the tick, four arms — the pre-recorded prediction said it carried the majority); **pool geometry is dead** (8.8x pool variation moved term2 by 1.1%, withdrawing my own 23.8% claim entirely); and the order asymmetry itself. Three sessions were lost to my own instrument — an anchor bound with no tolerance over an interval that was timing four TileLang compiles, a B=1 run whose shape was printed and never asserted, and a ratio tolerance broken 67x by my own warm fix. The closure gate every arm reported green **could not fail**: `fwd` cancels and term 3 is defined as the remainder. Next arm is two processes, behind the P1 gate, ckl's call. — [errors/2026-09-08-six-card-sessions-and-the-defect-did-not-move.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-six-card-sessions-and-the-defect-did-not-move.md)
- **accept** — **the draft head's in-loop acceptance is 78.0% / 75.0% at ctx 2048/1024, closing 08-30's `pending-remote` from a run that already existed.** The 08-30 alignment fix left "does in-loop acceptance reach the probe's 84.4%, and does it clear the 66% break-even" waiting on a card. It did not need one: a verify tick of width `W` commits one token plus its accepted drafts, so `tok/fwd - 1` is the accepted count, and **at depth 1 there is exactly one draft** — the 09-04 depth sweep's `tok/fwd` of 1.78 / 1.75 *is* that draft's acceptance rate. **0.89–0.92x of the probe's 84.4%**, not the 55.8% that reopened the 08-29 verdict, clearing the break-even by 9–12 points. Only depth 1 answers it: a chain is sequential, and the sweep's `rungs` column shows deeper rows mixing tick shapes (depth 2 at ctx 2048 is `r2 x5 + r4 x161`). Identity checked against the run's own totals rather than assumed — `215 × 1.78 = 383` tokens against the declared `--tokens 128 × 3`, **0.3% apart**. **Does not reopen 09-04**: entering rung 4 costs 60% of a tick against the 23–30% acceptance buys, so depth 1 wins and block-parallel drafting stays rejected. — [errors/2026-08-30-draft-attention-sees-one-token.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-08-30-draft-attention-sees-one-token.md)

- **fix, partial** — **a 1-token final prefill chunk made the disk-tier publish unreachable: 14 prompt lengths spilled nothing.** `_last_prefill_boundary(n)` predicts where `_build_plan` ends the last prefill chunk, and `_finish_prefills` compares against it to pick the one publish that reaches disk. On `n = 65, 129, … 2561` it names a position no chunk ends at, so `last` is never true — silent, since prefill and decode are correct, every counter reads normally, and the only observable is a tier that never receives an entry. Cause: the existing `tail == 1` back-off fires on the chunk that *carries* the tail, and at n=65 the 64-alignment lands on 64, so the remainder is a chunk of its own with `short = 64 − 64 = 0`. Correctness fix, **no perf delta** — 21606 chunks over n=2..4000 either way, since a 17-token tail replaces a 16+1 pair. Test drives the real planner and is verified red with the fix removed. **Partial**: `budget` is `max_num_batched_tokens − len(decodes)`, so a shared tick still loses the spill; four further fixes were measured and rejected, one because a deferred publish would pair an entry's tokens with a longer prefix's GDN state. `Status: open` for the budget-dependent half. — [errors/2026-09-08-a-one-token-chunk-made-last-unreachable.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-a-one-token-chunk-made-last-unreachable.md)

- **reject, no revert** — **#271 costs the DRAM-tier cell 1.81–2.12x, and a hit's TTFT is linear in the tokens it did not match** (R² 0.998, residual sd 0.81%, and it predicts the one unfitted miss row to −6.8%). Cutting prefill publishing from 62 entries per 31k prompt to 2 was correct on the flood — 62 entries against a 6-snapshot budget is a publisher that evicts itself. Measured on the **parent-child pair** `45acd87` → `a43a379` (16 lines of `engine.py`): 198.47 s against three child runs at 403.01 / 373.14 / 372.92 s, **100% of it TTFT**, per-hit cost 0.715 s → 9.9–10.3 s. `prefix_hit_tokens` (added here) makes the mechanism measured rather than inferred and corrects its shape: depth is a **512×k ratchet in submission order** — cumulative 322560 = 512 × 35×36/2 exactly — so first+last leaves each session a match set by its **queue position**, earliest sessions worst at 1.7%. The sign flips inside the run: +259 s at turns 0–1 where the parent's 61 rungs match deep, −54 s at turn 2 where the parent's flood has evicted itself and the child's 2 entries survive; net −205 s, which is why the accept grid liked it. **No revert.** The per-hit **fixed** term is **1.2–2.5 s and weakly identified** (the data never approaches full match, so it is extrapolated 44% past the nearest point); extra forwards explain 0.15–0.39 s of it. So the fix's two sizing constraints **conflict** — `K × snapshot_bytes ≤ budget` gives K ≈ 1 here while the depth threshold admits K ≈ 4–11 — and pricing a ladder needs one run with turns held constant. Ten withdrawn readings are kept in the entry in order. — [errors/2026-09-08-four-mechanisms-one-regression-and-a-copied-flag.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-four-mechanisms-one-regression-and-a-copied-flag.md)
- **reject** — **`compiles: clean` was vacuous on every cell of the 09-08 DRAM grid: four green verdicts, four 0-byte serve logs.** `_compiles` returned 0 for an empty-but-existing file, so `known` held and the verdict printed clean. The logs are empty by construction — the arms run `python3 -c ... > log` with no `-u`, so stdout block-buffers to a file, and each arm ends with `kill $SRV`. Measured on the pod with `-u` as the only variable: buffered leaves **0 bytes after 3 s and 0 after SIGTERM**, `python3 -u` leaves 38. `TILELANG_PRINT_ON_COMPILATION` defaults to `"1"`, so the marker is emitted and the empty log is the instrument, not a compile-free run. A JIT inside a measured turn would have been charged to the tier and read as clean. Fixed: an empty log reads `unknown`, while a populated log with no marker still reads `clean` so the verdict stays reachable. — [errors/2026-09-08-four-mechanisms-one-regression-and-a-copied-flag.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-four-mechanisms-one-regression-and-a-copied-flag.md)

## 2026-09-07

- **accept** — **the fp8 KV pool lands behind a default-off flag: 1.969x capacity, measured slower in both phases that were measured.** One f32 scale per `(plane, block, kv_head, token)`, which the writer's launch shape decides rather than accuracy — `T.Kernel(B*S, H)` cannot reduce a per-`(block, head)` absmax in one launch, and a per-block scale re-rounds every partial-block append. Writers and readers convert together because the pool alone was a regression: `kv_layer()` dequantized the whole plane per call, 64.49 ms against `kv_operands()`'s 0.04 ms for one tick, scaling with `num_blocks` instead of the sequence. On the 27B: 33280 bytes/token against 65536, 24/24 greedy tokens identical, and at B=32 x 32k it fits 89179 blocks where bf16 fits 45294 and queues 10 of the 32 requests. It is also **slower in both measured phases** — ~7% on prefill at B=8 ctx=8k, 1.73x on the whole B=32 run — because every gather casts and multiplies per KV element. **Off by default** pending a decode-bound long-context case and long-run quality evidence; the one open measurement is the per-tick decode ratio at equal concurrency, bounded at 1.079x. — 473 passed — [wins/2026-09-07-fp8-kv-pool-per-token-scales.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-07-fp8-kv-pool-per-token-scales.md)
- **accept** — **the prefix cascade is fixed at the publisher, not by a better victim: a prompt publishes 2 entries at any length, +28% reuse, and six eviction policies are refuted with the two best numbers among them being bugs.** A miss prefills from token 0 and every interior chunk boundary published one entry, so the count grew with prompt length — **4 at 2048 tokens, 16 at 8192, 62 at 31k against a budget holding 6** — and one miss evicted every other session's shared head, so 11 of 12 sessions missed in sequence at 14.1 s each on the H20. `_finish_prefills` now publishes the FIRST interior boundary and the last, a **constant 2** whatever the length. **The count is the operand, so no policy can fix it**: a policy chooses which of 62 entries to drop when 6 fit, and only a length-independent count gets under a fixed budget — which also refutes the every-2nd arm despite its better grid score, since 9 publishes at 8192 tokens is ~32 at 31k — the stride arms' grid figures are monkeypatched and rank the arms only, while the verdict against them rests on the publish count, which is arithmetic and harness-independent. Grid over shared-head length x snapshot budget, reuse read at admission, 196608 possible: **104448 against pure LRU's 81408 (+28%) with the eviction policy untouched**, where the six policies gave 83968 (two-class w=4), 83968 (extensions-first + reparent), 101376 (extensions-first longest), 109568 (capped sharers), 110592 (middles-first), 115712 (length x sharers) — **and the top two of those were artefacts**: 130560 for extensions-first came from dropping a middle entry and leaving its children pointing at a dead eid, so orphans read as family roots and got protected (closing the chain gives 83968), and 115712 for `length x sharers` collapses to **64 against LRU's 1152, -94%**, once stale formerly-hot prefixes exist, because `sharers` only rises — LFU pollution the 4x4 grid cannot see, since every prefix in it is live. **No decay rate repairs that**: increment per match with decay per eviction was swept at four half-lives and the rate that clears pollution drops the live head while the rate that keeps the head pollutes, monotonically. **The cost is cross-session partial reuse and it GROWS with prompt length**, so it is stated as an axis rather than a figure: followers sharing 50% of the lead prompt reuse 100.0% of ideal at 1024 tokens, **83.3% at 2048, 50.0% at 4096, 25.0% at 8192, 12.5% at 16384 and 6.4% at the H20 cell's own 31767** — the first interior boundary sits at one chunk (`max_num_batched_tokens`, 512) and does not move with the prompt, so every published prefix lives in the first ~2048 tokens and a long prompt's sharer matches an absolute cap of 3072 tokens rather than a fraction. The fix is length-independent in its COUNT, which is what the flood argument needs, and length-dependent in its VALUE. The publish count stays 2 on every row, asserted rather than eyeballed. The first boundary's depth is `max_num_batched_tokens`, config that serve never passes (so 512 is the shipped value these rows were measured at), and raising it to 2048 moves the cost rather than removing it — 16384 improves to 50.0% while a 2048-token prompt falls to **0.0%** because a prompt at one chunk has no interior boundary at all; the forward and tick-padding cost of a bigger chunk is unmeasured, so this is an observation about where the cost falls, not a default change. **The length-aware alternative is refuted and it is the more informative result**: K evenly spaced publishes fixes the length axis outright (100.0% at every length) and scores **81408 on the multi-session grid, pure LRU's baseline to the token**, giving back the entire cross-session gain — more publishes per row is more pressure on the shared head, the original cascade in miniature. Its 100.0% was itself an artifact of testing share fraction 0.5, which lands on a K=4 band edge at every power-of-two length; at 0.37/0.61/0.93 the same rule reads 78.8/88.8/87.4%. It also published 4 entries at a 3-snapshot budget (K ignores the budget, so the flood returns small) and K+1 rather than K at 31767 tokens. The remaining cost is a recompute, not a wrong answer, and the DRAM tier absorbs it where partial sharing is heavy — a claim the same H20 cell has to re-check, since the tier's measured 3.57x was priced against a publisher emitting 62 entries and the flood may be most of what it was relieving — **unpaid, not hidden**: the live V100 child has `prefix_hits 0` / `prefix_published 0` after 7+ hours, so the real share distribution is unavailable and this arm ships on the flood argument alone. The grid cannot price it either — its turn-2 request re-sends its own whole prompt and matches the completion publish, which is why the publish-nothing arm scored 180224 there while destroying **all** partial sharing (0 of 24576). **Two mutants gate it**, because the count assertion alone went GREEN against publish-last-only (also a constant 1, better on any self-hit fixture, and it costs every partial sharer): publish-every-boundary and publish-last-only are both red, at two prompt lengths 4x apart since a count that is small at one length proves nothing. Two vacuous instruments are recorded with it — a `lookup` read after the request finished scores a hit the row just published for itself (it made LRU appear to reuse 2064 tokens/session against the fix's 512), and a monkeypatched harness keyed on `id(pf)` aliased recycled ids and reported **+38% / -23% where the tree measures +28% / -17% at the same 2048-token fixture**, pessimistic on the gain and optimistic on the cost at once. #252 stays `xfail(strict)`: it calls `store.insert` directly, so it is the flood hand-written and unreachable from the publisher. 473 passed, 15 skipped, 1 xfailed — [wins/2026-09-08-cut-the-prefill-publish-flood.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-08-cut-the-prefill-publish-flood.md) · [errors/2026-09-08-the-eviction-policy-was-the-wrong-layer.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-08-the-eviction-policy-was-the-wrong-layer.md)

- **phase exit** — **the GRPO step is rollout-dominated at gen 1024: rollout 73.8%, the whole backward bucket 26.1%, and "backward_secs" was 19.7% forward.** Two numbers priced every RL lever and neither was what its name said. `rl_step` timed forward + loss + backward as one bucket called `backward_secs` (its own comment at `train.py:337` admitted the forward was in it), and the denominator came from [a16ff9c](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-06-one-grpo-step-is-54-percent-backward.md) — a tree from before the chunk ladder. Measured warm on card 0 at `73433bb`, `qwen38-27b` gen 1024 group 8 micro 1 tp=1: **step 85.617 s = rollout 63.156 (73.77%) + forward 4.415 (5.16%) + backward-only 17.957 (20.97%) + optimizer 0.087 + invalidate 0.001 + other 0.002**, reconstructing to **0.00e+00 s**. **a16ff9c's ruling "the next lever is backward, not the rollout" is now false**: at the same shape the rollout agrees to **5%** (63.156 vs 59.965) while the backward bucket is **0.313x** — 3.2x faster, because #229's chunk 16→64 at 1.94x, #234's 64→128, #240's fp4-backward-on-Ampere-MMA and #263's fp8 dX all landed after it. So 10% off the backward is 2.6% of the step against 7.4% for the rollout. **The GDN port's ceiling moved without the port changing**: `design-gdn-backward-port.md:11` called 1.470x "on the step" when it is a ratio on the bucket — on the measured step it is **1.091x**, against 1.211x had the bucket still been 54.4%; a lever priced against an old profile is priced against a denominator that no longer exists. **A mislabelled denominator is a sign problem, not a precision problem** (framing owed to v100-sm70-fp4-55): a ratio *R* on this bucket corrects by `1/(1-s/(1-F))` on the backward and `1/(1-s/F)` on the forward, opposite directions, so two levers priced against it are not comparable unless they touch the same half — 1.043x is 1.054x backward or 1.269x forward. Neither standing verdict flips (GDN adjoint stays reject, fp8 dX stays accept; both are backward levers). **F is stable warm 19.7% / cold 19.5%**, which is what rules out a forward-side kernel JIT-ing inside the timed window. Step 1's rollout was 660.1 s — **11.0x** a16ff9c's warm figure, all decode-kernel first-compile inside the timed region, not a regression. The instrument: `forward_secs` carved at the `no_grad`/tape seam with `_sync(backend)` between (without it the forward's launches are billed to whatever syncs first), `other_secs` derived, `invalidate_secs` timed, `backward_secs` kept additive for `manifest["metrics"]` readers. **The gate's first version was vacuous**: a 1% tolerance passed the mutant that zeroed `other_secs`, because the remainder is 0.054% of the tiny step — a band 18x looser than the term it guarded. Now exact; two mutants fail it. 472 passed, 15 skipped, 1 xfailed — [wins/2026-09-07-the-step-is-74-percent-rollout.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-07-the-step-is-74-percent-rollout.md)

- **accept** — **TP=2 for training works on hardware at no step penalty: 0.95x, and bucketing/overlap are not scheduled.** First GPU execution of our collectives. The gate passes on real NCCL — `production Backend world=2 on cuda/nccl`, sharded CE 2.161235 against unsharded, bit-identical to the gloo run — and it checks three things no CPU run can: the comm selection at `backend.py:177` (which `tests/tp_world2.py` cannot reach, `RefBackend` hardcodes gloo), the device-index binding from #266 (the same code on one card gave NCCL's `Duplicate GPU detected` this morning), and `cross_entropy_loss_grad`'s dispatch through a real process group. Four arms, one session, one content sha: **tp=2 10.038 s/step against tp=1's 10.596** — two cards marginally FASTER while sharding every matmul and adding 2696 collectives — with per-card peak **43.97 → 25.46 GiB**. **Two corrections to the attribution, both load-bearing.** `all_reduce` reads **8 µs/call, below the 20.6 µs NCCL floor**: not an artifact and not a dropped collective, its backward is `yield 0, g` (`autograd.py:225`) because a row-parallel forward already leaves `dY_r = dY` on every rank, so the row is tape bookkeeping and **99% of collective time is `tp_fork`**, 1672 calls at 0.7371 s. And the two ranks differ **3x on that same op** (0.7371 vs 0.2402 s, same call count) — an all-reduce makes the early rank wait, so the 0.745 s is an upper bound twice over and part of it is skew, not comms; someone optimizing "the collective" optimizes a wait, and rank 1's 0.2402 s (2.39% of the step) is the closer estimate of what TP comms cost. **Bucketing recovers 0.34%, not 6%**: at 0.441 ms/call `tp_fork` runs **21.4x the 20.6 µs floor**, so 1672 × 20.6 µs = 34.4 ms is 4.7% of the op — the time is inside the calls, not in launching them, and fusing calls does not remove it. With `linear_attn_chunk` alone at 2.87–3.22 s, the comms items come off the schedule. **A first TP run pays a full JIT rebuild, per rank**: step 1 **456.19 s** against step 2's 10.04, because both ranks compile independently and the shard shapes miss a `tilelang_cache` warm for months of single-card runs — 500+ compiles before the first step, and it invalidated my own 4 × 8m33s window estimate, which came from the cache-warm control arm. Provenance is the content sha `016ab33b5f36` (profiler + `src/` + kernels), identical across all four arms, because the pod tree is not a git repo and the header printed `HEAD unknown`. Also in: the six stale lines in `design-tp-on-hardware.md` deferred from #266, and the roadmap's TP items. 468 passed, 10/10 distributed gates — [wins/2026-09-07-tp2-on-two-cards.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-07-tp2-on-two-cards.md)

- **reject** — **the GDN backward adjoint port is cut on its own numbers: 3.102 s of a 22.145 s backward, 1.043x on the BACKWARD at the one measured op-speedup class.** The port was scoped against `linear_attn_chunk` at **45.300 s / 65.7%** of attributed. That figure is stale by a config, not by code: `_GDN_CHUNK` was 16 when it was measured and has shipped at 128 since #234 (f0e6e71 16->64, 88e3764 64->128 — five lines in `reference.py`, the constant and its comment; `kernels_gdn.py` and `autograd.py` have **zero diff** across the 44 commits between the shas). Call count is **384 in both runs**, so the work is identical and the whole factor is per-call, 117.969 -> 16.551 ms; the `--gdn-chunk` flag did not exist at the old sha, so that run took the shipped 16. Mechanism: the constant is the sequential dimension of both python loops in `gdn_backward`, 80 iterations per layer-call at C=16 against 10 at C=128, both measured dispatch-bound. Not the instrument — identical call count means identical sync count, and the absolute unattributed residual is 4.797 s against 4.907 s, 2.3% apart. Re-measured on one tree, three arms in one job on card 0: bare **22.145**, registry 25.649 (attributed 20.848 / 21936 calls), `--inside-gdn` 24.792 (attributed 7.188). **#219's 55/35/10 bracket was a C=16 measurement and is superseded**: at C=128 the adjoint is **43.16%** of the GDN row (3.102 s), recompute 25.94%, and the chunk-invariant prologue/epilogue **doubled to 24.82%** because it runs once per layer-call whichever the chunk. So phase 1's numerator is 3.102 s, not 0.55 x 7.249, and at the 1.411x measured for the fp8 dX kernel it buys **1.043x** — the same as that kernel bought for one gradient instead of six, and the same order as the fp8 tile/thread sweep already cut for a ~1 s ceiling. **No backward-side op lever clears 1.1x on the backward**: the top row is the whole GDN row at 1.091x, every other at or under 1.070x. Every ratio here is a backward ratio; none is a step ratio. Also read and rejected in the same pass: the prologue/epilogue is **92 eager tensor ops per call**, not bandwidth — its conv1d recompute plus both `repeat_interleave` materialisations are 0.32 ms at 2 TB/s against a measured 4.645 ms — so the win there is fusing many small ops behind a gradient gate each, capped at 1.084x if all 1.784 s vanished. The step denominator stays **unmeasured**: these arms run no rollout (`train_secs` 25.728 against `backward_secs` 25.649 is a 0.08 s optimizer), so every gain above is on the backward and the rollout share is not priced. Measurement only, no runtime change — [wins/2026-09-07-where-the-backward-goes-at-c128.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-07-where-the-backward-goes-at-c128.md)
- **accept** — **every rank of a torchrun launch bound card 0, and NCCL says so itself: "Duplicate GPU detected : rank 1 and rank 0 both on CUDA device".** `LOCAL_RANK` was read nowhere in `src/` or `packages/` and `torch.cuda.set_device` only in two scripts, so `Backend.__init__`'s `torch.cuda.current_device()` gave every rank the same card — the TP training stack has been correct on gloo/CPU since 09-05 and had never run on a GPU. Four lines fix it, placed **before** `self.device`, which `init_tp`'s `comm = "nccl" if self.device.type == "cuda" else "gloo"` reads; the range test `0 <= local < device_count()` covers both launcher shapes, no-torchrun (absent) and a launcher that already pinned one card per rank (past the count). **`tests/tp_world2.py` drives `RefBackend`, which hardcodes gloo**, so at world=2 nothing exercised the real comm selection, collectives through a real process group, or the **dispatch** in `Backend.cross_entropy_loss_grad` — the `tp_rank * vloc` offset and the `group=self._tp_pg` closure, distinct from `reference.cross_entropy_sharded`, which `ce_sharded_world2.py` already gates. `tests/tp_backend_world2.py` does, with two controls that fail on their own assertions (`--no-collective` makes all_reduce the identity; `--rank0-shard` is exactly the offset bug, loss 2.161 → 2.549) and a cuda-only third that fires on two cards. **The one-card window found two defects in the test and one in the launcher, none of which a two-card run would have shown apart from the measurement**: the gate's probes were host tensors and nccl has no CPU backend (`No backend type associated with device type cpu`), and every `pod_run.sh` launch was printing `pod_run_claim: command not found` because `RUNNER_EOF` is unquoted and a backticked word in a runner comment is a command substitution **evaluated on the laptop** — the emitted runner stayed valid and the card was still claimed, so the only witness was stderr, which the selftest did not capture and now does as arm 0 (checked against the mutant). **world=2 over NCCL is impossible on one visible card by construction** — `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0` makes `device_count()` 1, rank 1 falls out of range, and the binding correctly declines; the gate now skips with that sentence instead of dying inside NCCL. 27B tp=1 bare on card 0: **22.09 s/step backward, 62.62 GiB peak**, the reference the tp=2 arms are read against — cross-session, so `tp_step_arms.py` re-runs both arms in one session when the second card arrives. 468 passed, 10/10 distributed gates. The NCCL gate and the tp=2 arms are pending-remote with their commands — [wins/2026-09-07-every-rank-bound-card-zero.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-07-every-rank-bound-card-zero.md)

- **accept** — **the DRAM snapshot tier is 3.57x on wall clock inside its own pressure regime, and the SSD layer under it served 0 of 108 offers while costing 1.85x.** The tier's condition is `sessions > resident snapshots` and the numerator had no flag until `--state-bytes` (f038c96): the budget came only from `engine.py:1606`, a quarter of free memory, which on an H20 is 17.9 GiB = **116 snapshots** at the measured 156.9 MiB, so every prior arm read `0` promotions from outside the regime rather than inside it. Four cells, 12 concurrent conversations each opening with the same ~30k system prefix, `qwen38-27b` NVFP4 on H20 card 0, all four `compiles: clean`: **1.0 GiB / 6 snapshots tier off 199.35 s, dram 55.82 s (3.57x), dram+ssd 103.00 s, and 2.0 GiB / 13 snapshots tier off 59.38 s** as the control. The store's own `prefix_entries_capacity` reads **6** and **13** against the two budgets, confirming the arithmetic independently rather than restating it. **The control is what makes it the budget and not the workload**: at 13 snapshots the tier-off arm gets 12/12 turn-2 hits, so cell 1's 199 s is what a 6-snapshot budget does to 12 sessions. Cell 1 loses **11 of 12** hits at turn 2 and pays 14.1 s of prefill each, 835 evictions; cell 2 has **0** evictions and 12/12, its 180 demotions moving snapshots to the host instead of dropping them. Cell 2 also **beats the unpressured control** 1.06x, because a demote-promote costs less than the control's own 190 evict-and-reprefills. **The SSD layer is inert, and its counters say so before its wall clock does**: cell 3's hits/promotions/demotions are byte-identical to cell 2's (35/24/180), with `ssd_hits: 0`, `ssd_recovered: 0`, **`ssd_save_ms: 64768`** and 19.5 GB written — and because the saves sit in the demote path they slowed the layer above, `dram_demote_ms` 4883 → **8428** for identical work. Reproduces [the 1.65x serve-path finding](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-06-the-ssd-tier-is-165x-worse-at-12-sessions.md) at another budget with the cost now attributed to a counter. **The DRAM default does NOT flip**: 3.57x is measured and licenses a pressured card, while 116 snapshots is derived arithmetic — an H20 at its shipped default needs ~115 concurrent sessions to enter the regime, so the two verdicts are kept apart. The card where the shipped default IS pressured is the V100, and that one is **read, not derived**: `/health` on the live child reports `prefix_state_bytes_budget` 1845067776 = **1.718 GiB** and `prefix_entries_capacity` **11** at a 149.6 MiB snapshot, so 12 sessions is past it by one — an earlier draft said 9 from 8 GiB at 144 MiB and **both operands were wrong** (8 GiB is `PrefixStore`'s own default, not the quarter-of-free this card gets after pools; 144 MiB is the bf16 snapshot, not this checkpoint's), two errors composing into a plausible number. There the tier is HBM→SSD with no host layer (`kv_cache.py:390-396`) — the arm measured worst here. Cell 1 was first run cold at 250.14 s with 12 compiles in measured turns and **re-run warm rather than reported**; the re-run also refuted the neighbour-row way of pricing them, where three estimates agreed within 1.3% and a fourth was wrong by 2.3 s/compile because its neighbour was a prefix hit and the dirty row a miss — **agreement among estimates that share a premise tests the estimates, not the premise** (raised by tilerl-48 before the warm run landed). Also in: `pod_run_claim` reads card_claim's exit code instead of the word `claimed`, after the substring test killed a healthy 27B server holding a valid claim — enumerated rather than imagined, **three** of `acquire`'s success returns lack the word — `:887` ancestor-shares-no-claim-written, `:1046` `claim reused, not re-taken`, `:639` grant-lane — against one at `:1085` that has it — and the selftest's mock, which printed real refusal messages while exiting 0 for all of them, now carries the real exit codes so its kill-control arm cannot go green against a runner that never kills. [wins/2026-09-07-the-dram-tier-is-357x-when-the-budget-is-pressured.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-07-the-dram-tier-is-357x-when-the-budget-is-pressured.md) · [wins/2026-09-07-the-state-budget-had-no-flag.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-07-the-state-budget-had-no-flag.md) · [errors/2026-09-07-a-reused-claim-read-as-a-refusal.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-07-a-reused-claim-read-as-a-refusal.md)
- **accept** — **the NCCL all-reduce floor is 20.6 µs, the 21.5 µs that ten sites consume was right to 4%, and the run proving it sat on the pod a week before the entry saying it did not exist.** `scripts/nccl_probe.py` was written, never cited, and [declared unrun](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-06-the-nccl-floor-has-no-instrument.md) — true of the tree, false of the machine: `/work/nccl{,6,6b,6c}.log`, dated 2026-08-29, are four completed runs at world=8 and world=6. Measured now at **world=2 on an idle 0+1 pair, three repeats: 20.6 / 20.7 / 21.4 µs** at 20 KB, 21.4–22.0 at 1.3 MB, 53.8–55.0 at 10 MB (190 GB/s). **"Flat 20 KB → 1.3 MB" holds** — 64x the bytes for **4% more time** — which is the second claim the entry flagged as untested, and it means a small collective is pure latency and call count, not message size, is the lever. **The 108 µs outlier that would have reopened a shipped design decision was contention**: `nccl6b.log` reads 4.7x its neighbours at 163840 bytes and `nccl6.log` 3x at 20480, while the same sizes in the sibling runs read 22.4 / 22.6 / 22.9 with cards 2-7 held by another team — one anomalous cell is not a finding until the repeat says so. No table needed revising; the nine prose citations in `design-parallel.md` and `roadmap.md` are re-pointed from a CHANGELOG line that *consumed* the figure to the measurement that produces it, and the roadmap's derived ~2 ms becomes 2.64 ms (128 × 20.6). **The probe never needed 8 cards** — it is world-agnostic and only its docstring said `--nproc_per_node=8`, which is why nobody ran it. Also in: `prof_backward_ops.py` threads `tp=`/`backend` into `_build_model`, without which a torchrun launch profiles an unsharded step per rank with no collective at all, and `pod_run.sh` takes a card list with per-card orphan refusal. [wins/2026-09-07-the-nccl-floor-was-measured-a-week-early.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-07-the-nccl-floor-was-measured-a-week-early.md)
