There is no JS execution anywhere in CI, which was true before this and still is. **A string is not code until something runs it** — ruff lints the Python around `_CHAT_UI`, pytest imports it, git diffs it, and a call to an undefined function is the most basic error a language can report with nothing in the pipeline positioned to report it. And the reason I missed it: **I verified the change on the wire and called it verified.** "197 passed, ruff clean" was true, the SSE stream was measured on the card, deltas counted, Chinese checked for replacement chars — all of it upstream of the broken line. **A gate that passes tells you what it covers, not that the change works.** Same pass: the page is redesigned to the low-saturation brief (max saturation 32%, warm-grey with one pine accent, every old saturated value gone; measured contrast AA in both themes), reasoning folds into a `<details>` that auto-collapses when `</think>` arrives, agent events encode kind structurally rather than by hue, and a stop button lands. Served rate re-measured on the card after the restart: **34.0 tok/s, three runs identical** (81 tokens, prompt 58, real `completion_tokens` from the stream's usage — not the `chars/4` guess). Right-sized the demo server's pool at the same time (`--blocks 2048` → `512`, the 289 a 4K request needs plus slack): **resident memory fell 28.8 → 25.6 GB**, matching the 3.9 GB over-reservation computed in the block-cost entry. 197 passed, 4 skipped, ruff clean. [errors/2026-09-03-addthinking-was-never-defined.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-addthinking-was-never-defined.md)

- **fix** — **the served prompt did not match the checkpoint's own chat template, and the page's tok/s was fabricated.** Found by the user opening the chat page: `<think>` rendered as answer text and the rate meter was wrong. `render_chat`'s default left the assistant turn bare — neither opening nor closing `<think>` — and **that state does not exist in `chat_template.jinja`**: it treats an undefined `enable_thinking` as **true** (line 46) and always emits one of exactly two tails (165-168). So the model was handed a prompt position it has never seen ending there and did the locally likely thing: **emitted `<think>` as its own first token**. We also never rendered the reasoning-effort instructions the template injects as a system turn (45-56). **Rendered with jinja2 on the pod rather than read**, and all four modes now match byte for byte: default = xhigh instructions + `<think>\n`; `low` = its own instructions; **`medium` is accepted and deliberately carries none**; `enable_thinking: false` = the closed empty block and no system turn; a caller's own system turn gets the instructions **prepended inside it**, not a second turn; unknown effort raises as the template raises. `None` stays for the tiny/dev path — `ByteTokenizer` has no `<think>` token — and the server now picks the default by **asking the tokenizer** (`<think>` is a single id **248068** in the real vocab, 7 raw bytes under ByteTokenizer) instead of by default argument. Second half: `_chat_chunk` hard-coded `"usage": None`, so the page estimated `chars/4` — **about 4× low for Chinese, where one character is roughly one token**, i.e. a fabricated number on the demo's own meter. Now a final usage chunk carries the engine's `len(output_ids)`, **opt-in behind `stream_options.include_usage`** because it has no `choices` and an unconditional one **broke two existing tests** (a client indexing `choices[0]` every frame raises on it). Page also gained conversation history, a folded thinking block built by re-partitioning the accumulated text each frame (the tags land mid-delta), and an AbortController for stop. **A template is executable, so execute it** — both halves of the defect are things the jinja states plainly and that I would have paraphrased wrongly: that undefined means *on* rather than *neutral*, and that thinking-on injects a system turn at all. And: **the `None` state was invented for a test double, then became the default every real client got** — the dev path's compromise silently became production behaviour. Not yet measured on the card: the corrected prompt changes what the model sees, so served rate and acceptance could both move; the recorded B=1 curve is unaffected (the bench path never calls `render_chat`). 197 passed, 4 skipped, ruff clean. [errors/2026-09-03-served-prompt-did-not-match-the-checkpoint-template.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-served-prompt-did-not-match-the-checkpoint-template.md)

- **verdict** — **a KV block costs 2.125 MiB on sm70, not the 0.92 MB the bench printed for months; 2.42× low, pinned by a test.** No runtime change and nothing ever failed — that is the finding. `bench_ctx_decode.py` sized *and printed* its pool at 0.92 MB/block; built from the shipped pool classes the way `build_engine` does (`engine.py:1191` trunk, `:358` the draft's mirrored plane) it is **2.0000 + 0.1250 = 2.1250 MiB (2.2282 MB)** at 16 full-attn planes × 4 kv heads × 16 tokens × 256 head_dim × 4 B for k and v. **0.92 was two errors compounded**: a bf16 figure (bf16 is 1.0625 MiB) *and* missing the draft plane — the old `0.79 + 0.13` decomposition tracks the bf16 shape, so it predates the pool moving to f32 IO, which is deliberate (`engine.py:1202`: a bf16 pool made every attention call cast the whole plane, 14% of a 4096-ctx token). **Why it was invisible: the number describes runs, it does not make them work.** The print exists specifically so two runs both reporting a "ctx=512" row can be compared for free memory — the comparison that stalled a run in the expandable-segments entry — and it was off by 2.42× the whole time. Concrete cost: the demo server I started with `--blocks 2048` reserved **4.56 GB where I reported 1.9 GB**, against a workload needing **289 blocks (644 MB)** for one request at ctx=4096 plus a 512-token completion — **7× over-reserved**, fitting inside 28.7 GB so it produced no symptom, only a smaller headroom than stated. A second stale constant in the same comment ("ctx 4096 at B=4 needs 1060" → now **1092**, since `--tokens` moved 32→128) is replaced with the formula instead of another number that will rot. **A constant in a comment is a claim with no gate on it** — pinned now in `tests/test_kv.py` at the real 27B config, negative control verified. Narrower rule: **`0.79 + 0.13` looked measured.** A decomposed constant reads as more trustworthy than a round one, which is why a stale decomposition survives longer; this one was right about the structure (trunk + draft plane) and wrong about the dtype, so checking the structure confirmed it. 194 passed, 4 skipped, ruff clean, no GPU. [errors/2026-09-03-block-cost-was-2.4x-low-in-the-harness.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-block-cost-was-2.4x-low-in-the-harness.md)

- **accept** — **incremental SSE ships, and the previous entry's diagnosis was wrong: the blocker was my own poll loop, not the engine lock** (`engine.peek` + `_stream`). The reject below concluded that streaming needs `step()`'s `_lock` narrowed — a correctness change to the engine core with the tape and KV pools downstream. **The lock was never touched.** Two errors in that attribution: (1) **`peek` does not need the lock at all** — under the GIL the writer's `req.output.append` (`engine.py:1082`) and a reader's `list(req.output)` are each one bytecode, so the copy is always a consistent prefix; the reverted version took `_lock`, which is exactly what made it unobservable. (2) **the poll loop called `take()` every iteration**, and `take()` *does* take the lock, so a lock-free `peek` was queued behind a call that blocks for a whole forward. Instrumented rather than argued — `scripts/probe_sse_deltas.py` counts the calls: same `peek`, `take()` inside the loop gives **1 delta / 2 polls**, `take()` only after `peek` returns None gives **3 deltas / 15 polls**. `peek` returns None once the request has left `_waiting`+`_running`, which by construction means `_finish` has already filed it, so `take()` will answer — that is what lets the stream poll without touching the lock until the run is over. Two UTF-8 rules, both load-bearing: decode the **whole prefix** each poll (one token can be a partial sequence), and **cut at the first U+FFFD** and hold the rest, because a prefix can still end mid-character. **On the V100, streaming costs nothing measurable**: 128 tokens at temperature 0, **stream 32.2 / 32.4 / 32.4 tok/s against non-stream 32.3 / 32.5** — inside the 1.16% noise floor, and 0.9% from the recorded ctx=32 B=1 figure of 32.7, so the instrument is not lying about either axis. Chinese survives whole: 5 deltas, **zero U+FFFD mid-stream**. Stated ceiling: **6 deltas per 128 tokens is one per ~21 tokens** where a 20 ms poll has ~197 chances, so the short lock paths and the GIL still serialize most of it — narrowing `step()`'s lock remains the lever if per-token is ever wanted, and the code carries that as its ponytail marker. **When an accessor cannot see shared state, suspect the reader before the writer** — the cheap side of a contention problem is the side you control. The `strict=True` xfail did its job by forcing the marker to be re-examined instead of aging into scenery, but its *reason* asserted a cause I had not exhausted. What the CPU gate can and cannot prove: it checks the split points (>1 delta, joined == non-streamed, no U+FFFD **mid-stream**), while the final delta is exempt because `tiny()`'s random bytes are not valid UTF-8 at all — only the V100 run proves real multi-byte text survives. 193 pass, 4 skipped, ruff clean. [wins/2026-09-03-incremental-sse-was-a-starved-poll-loop.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-incremental-sse-was-a-starved-poll-loop.md)

- **reject** — **incremental SSE is blocked by the engine lock, not by a missing accessor; attempted, reverted, recorded as a strict xfail.** A single-user demo needs the viewer to see tokens arrive, and `server.py:245` emits the whole completion as ONE SSE delta (its ponytail marker says incremental streaming "needs an engine event stream (day-2)"). The first half of my read was right — `_Req.output` (`engine.py:134`) is a live per-tick list and `_await_completion` already polls at 20 ms, so no event system is needed, just an accessor plus a text-delta loop, ~35 lines. **The half I did not check first was whether a reader can run at all while a tick is in flight, and it cannot**: `step()` holds `_lock` across `_build_plan` AND `_run_forward` (`engine.py:557`), and the daemon loop calls it back to back. Measured under the real driver: one `take()` call **blocked 325 ms of a 335 ms generation**, and a 20 ms poll loop achieved **3 polls for 24 tokens**; `peek()` returned `None` on **100%** of calls. The same accessor works perfectly when `step()` is driven by hand (1,2,…,11 then None) — which is exactly what made the change look finished, so: **driving a loop by hand is not the concurrency the code runs in**. Both files restored to HEAD rather than shipping a `peek` that always returns `None` with a `_stream` written to consume it. What the next attempt actually needs: release `_lock` around `_run_forward` while keeping `_build_plan` atomic, then establish that a concurrent `submit` cannot mutate `_running`/`_waiting` under a forward reading its planned rows and that `take`/`_finish` cannot pop mid-tick — a correctness change to the engine core, not a demo tweak. Kept as the artifact: a test asserting the two properties that pull against each other (**more than one** content delta AND the joined deltas equal the non-streamed text with no U+FFFD, using the byte tokenizer so any multi-byte character is guaranteed to span tokens), marked `xfail(strict=True)` with the lock measurement in its reason — the existing `test_completion_stream` passes with one delta and can see neither the bug nor the fix. **A marker that names the wrong obstacle costs more than no marker**: "needs an engine event stream" sent me at the observability API when the obstacle was six lines away in `step()`. 192 pass, 1 xfailed, ruff clean, no GPU used. [errors/2026-09-03-sse-streaming-is-blocked-by-the-engine-lock.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-sse-streaming-is-blocked-by-the-engine-lock.md)

- **baseline** — **single-stream V100 tops out at 45.9 tok/s (ctx=1024), the tick is flat across context, and the last three commits are worth zero at B=1.** First V100 B=1 context curve on this branch, run because the target changed from throughput to one concurrent request — where the B=8 **88.5 tok/s is ~11 tok/s per request, BELOW the B=2 serve default's 20.9**, so the aggregate is a wrong answer rather than a rough one. B=1 depth 3: **32.7 / 43.5 / 45.9 / 39.8 / 34.7 tok/s** at ctx 32 / 512 / 1024 / 2048 / 4096. The rate peaks in the middle and **nothing got faster**: derived tick time is **64.3 / 65.8 / 62.4 / 65.8 / 69.7 ms — flat, 11.7% across a 128× context span** — so the entire curve is acceptance, `tok/forward` 2.10 → 2.86 → 2.86 → 2.62 → 2.42. That is the split-KV entry's "the context slope is gone" holding at B=1 on the real model. **The prediction committed before the run held on two axes**: ctx=32 reads **32.7 against the recorded 32.4 (+0.9%, inside the 1.16% noise floor)** and `tok/forward` reads **2.10 against a recorded 2.10, identical** — the second is the stronger half, since a rate can coincide but an acceptance count matching to the hundredth means the same tokens were drafted and accepted. The mechanism was named in advance from the code: B=1 depth 3 is 4 rows → the 4 rung, below `_NCOLS_MIN_M = 32` (`backend.py:511`), so `ncols=2` never engages; PO at one row is ~96-192 MiB, nowhere near the ceiling `70b53f2` and `a158672` removed; `last_only` reduces 4 rows, not 32. **So three commits of memory work bought single-stream nothing** — not a defect in them (they removed a B=8 ctx=512 OOM, a capacity result reported as one), but it means the single-stream path has had no attention on this branch and its levers are different. Record-keeping fixed while checking: **45.9 is the best measured V100 single-stream figure**; 52.6 / 74.9 / 87.5 / 90.9 are all **H20/sm90** and **87.5 must never be quoted as a V100 number** — the recorded sm70:sm90 ratio is 4.31×, logged as 97% of the two cards' bandwidth ratio, so most of the gap is the memory system. Harness fix that made the run honest (`61ee8c6`): `b = max(4, args.batch)` gave a single-request run `max_batch=4`, i.e. **990 MB of pool instead of 510 and 4× the decode graphs** for rows that could never be admitted; floored at 2, and **B ≥ 4 is byte-identical** so no earlier number moved. [wins/2026-09-03-single-stream-b1-baseline.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-single-stream-b1-baseline.md)

- **verdict** — **B=8 depth 3 tops out between ctx 512 and 1024, three buffers now OOM at the SAME padded shape, and the 88.5 tok/s needs an env var the tree never sets.** Three results, no runtime change. (1) `PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF=expandable_segments:True` is **load-bearing**: it appears nowhere in `src/`, `packages/` or any script — only in my pod runner — and without it the same build still OOMs, so the ctx=512 entry's number is scoped to "with the flag". Whether to ship it is **not** decided here: it changes allocator behaviour globally and its throughput cost on this workload is unmeasured. (2) **ctx=1024 does not run.** (3) The mechanism: three different allocations have now failed at **exactly 7 × 512 rows** — the attention partials (1.50 GiB), the fused `gate_up` output `y2` (**476 MiB**, `backend.py:500`), and `silu_mul`'s contiguous copy (**238 MiB**, `backend.py:1022`) — because `engine.py:729` gives every row in a tick one bucketed width. Each byte count decodes to that shape exactly: `476 MiB / 4 = 3584 × 34816` and `238 MiB / 4 = 3584 × 17408`, with `3584 = 7 × 512`. So halving a dtype moved the wall to the next allocation of the same shape, twice, and **the padding is the mechanism, not any buffer's dtype**. New finding on the way through: `gate`/`up` are **strided slices** of the `[M, 2I]` fused output (verified locally — `gu.is_contiguous()` True, both slices False), so `silu_mul` copies each half, putting one MLP layer's transient at **952 MiB (476 + 238 + 238)** — larger than the 768 MiB attention buffer two entries were spent halving. Two named levers, neither measured: have the fused GEMV write `[2, M, I]` so each half is contiguous (weight permutation is load-time, runtime cost zero, MLP-local), or bound `rows × width` — `max_num_batched_tokens` bounds only summed chunk length (`engine.py:563`, `:580`). **The cost was two instrument faults in my own guards, both firing on healthy runs**: `num_blocks` came from `max(ctxs)`, so `--max-ctx 1024` grew the pool 236 MB (568 blocks vs 312) and stalled its own ctx=512 step — a context that reads 88.5 when 512 tops the sweep; and a flat 300 s prefill deadline over work that is **254 s of legitimate prefill at ctx=1024** (8192 prompt tokens × 31 ms, 85% of budget) fired at "8/8 admitted" with nothing stuck. Fixed by `--min-ctx` (`85f76d7`) and `max(300, 4·batch·ctx·0.031)` (`3c9c704`); the run now prints the pool it sized. **A guard's threshold has to scale with what the guard is watching**, or it eventually measures itself and reports it in the vocabulary of a real failure. [errors/2026-09-03-expandable-segments-is-load-bearing.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-expandable-segments-is-load-bearing.md)

- **accept** — **B=8 at ctx=512 runs: the sm70 partials take f16 and the split count is now chosen per tick** (`70b53f2` + `a158672`). Two changes to the only allocation that scales with a tick's *padded* width. `PO [B, S, H, KVSPLIT, D]` goes f32→f16 (PM/PL stay f32 — they hold the log-domain max and logsum, and are 12 MiB together), and `KVSPLIT` stops being one hard-wired 32: `S < 8` takes 32, `S ≥ 8` takes 16, the threshold above the widest verify the ladder submits. **ctx=512 reads 88.5 tok/s where every earlier arm OOMed** (1.41 GiB in the draft readout → 1.50 GiB in the partials → 960 MiB → 1.31 GiB), and ctx=32 is **61.1 against a 61.1 baseline** — zero cost by construction, since every tick there is S≤4 and takes the same kernel it always took. **88.5 vs 61.1 is NOT a 1.45× speedup**: the ctx=512 tick is **1.063× SLOWER** (244.1 vs 229.6 ms) and the whole rate gain is acceptance, **tok/forward 14.00 → 21.60 (1.543×)** — the two multiplied give 1.451× against a measured 1.448×, **0.2% apart**, so none of it can be claimed for the kernel. What the work bought is that the row exists. **Parity passes on the card at both shipped split counts** (`parity OK (cuda) at KVSPLIT 32 and 16`), worst 3.424e-04 at ks32 and 1.493e-04 at ks16 against the *dense* kernel — 30× inside `rtol=1e-2` and the same order as the **2.1-2.6e-4 predicted before the run** from a simulation that also measured **max|PO| = 9.33 against f16's 65504** (the running max is already subtracted, so the values are O(1)). **My queued plan was refuted by its own measurement**: a flat flip to KVSPLIT=16 looked free on a recorded 1.17×, but that figure is **S=32 prefill width only** — swept at the widths a spec tick runs, 16 **loses 20% at S=1** (246.5 vs 205.1 µs) and is a wash at S=4 and S=32. The two constraints sit at opposite ends of S and never conflict: 32 earns its 20% where PO is 3 MiB, 16 is free where PO is 1.500 GiB, so *choose per tick* — the same host-static idiom `_paged_attention_decode` already uses for sm90. Also **withdrawn: my "the peak is 5 rows"**, published from `960 MiB ÷ per-row bytes`; the arithmetic was right and the reading wrong, because with more headroom the same run died at 7 rows — **a failed allocation names a shape, not the peak**. Two standing gaps found and recorded rather than widened: the split kernel **has no CPU twin** (`tl.reduce` unimplemented for `target="c"`; **negative control run, pristine HEAD fails identically**), and the parity gate **pinned KVSPLIT=16 while the registry shipped 32**, so it had never exercised the shipped value (fixed in `2dea2cb`, now one exported rule both the dispatch and its test call, `9a1eaf8`). **One number does not reproduce and I am naming the instrument**: recorded ks16 at S=32 was 4002, this run reads 4660 (16% slower) while ks32 reproduces to 0.4%; the only change between runs is PO f16. Unresolved; the within-run comparisons the design rests on are unaffected. 192 tests pass, ruff clean, two negative controls on the new width test verified. [wins/2026-09-03-attention-partials-in-f16.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-attention-partials-in-f16.md)


- **default flip** — **the draft's readout is reduced to the row it reads** (`16382c8`). `DraftHead.forward` gained `last_only` — the same per-row valid-length argument the trunk already takes, where `model.py:371` records "lm_head over every prefill position is 4.7% of the FLOPs and a 508 MB output thrown away". The draft's `forward` simply lacked it, so its readout was materialised vocab-wide in f32 at prefill width while `_draft_step` reads **one row per request: 8 rows of 248320 = 7.6 MiB against 1444 MiB allocated, 191×**. At B=8 ctx=512 PyTorch's allocation falls **27.39 → 25.86 GiB (−1.53)** and the readout is gone from the OOM traceback; at ctx=32 the tick goes **233.8 → 228.2 ms (1.8%)** with **`tok/forward` identical at 14.00** — ~768 draft decisions on the real 27B head with bit-identical acceptance, which is the parity that counts. **Correct but not sufficient**: ctx=512 still OOMs, now in `paged_attention_split`'s partials at **1.50 GiB — the same allocation already recorded for the B=4 ceiling**, so B=8 is pushed back to the B=4 wall rather than past it, and reserved-but-unallocated rose 1.67 → **2.82 GiB**, making "footprint or fragmentation" a separate testable question. Two of my own methods failed on the way: **the first negative control passed** — flipping the reduction to row 0 satisfied all four draft-parity tests, because their spy reads `out[i, -1]` *after* the reduction and so compares a wrong index against its own choice, so **a covered code path is not a discriminating input**; and a committed prediction was **right about the number, wrong about the mechanism** (I said a decode tick is T=1 so the reduction would be a no-op — it is T=1+depth=4 and fires on verify ticks too). Also fixed: `prof_spec_tick.py` submitted **one** request, so its attribution described the 4 rung, not the 32 rung serving compiles. [wins/2026-09-03-draft-readout-reduced-to-the-row-it-reads.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-draft-readout-reduced-to-the-row-it-reads.md)

- **verdict** — **`serve --max-batch 8` REJECTED: B=8's ceiling is ctx=32, and the cause is the draft head's f32 readout.** B=8 depth 3 OOMs at ctx=512 asking **1.41 GiB with 12.38 MiB free of 31.74**, and the traceback names the allocation rather than leaving it to be guessed: `_run_forward` → `_draft_step` (`engine.py:905`) → `DraftHead.forward` (`spec.py:117`) → `linear_fp4` → **`y2 = torch.empty(M, N, float32)`** (`backend.py:496`). So it is the **draft** — which runs on *every* tick including chunked prefill — at **prefill width** (`y2` exists only when `len(chunks) > 1`, i.e. M > 32), through a **vocab-wide f32** buffer. Three earlier guesses at the B=4 ceiling produced three wrong causes; this run was left to fail with its trace intact, and **a stack trace is a measurement where a plausible cause is not**. The rejection rests on three independent reasons, none of them throughput: OOM at ctx=512, **16 precapture graphs against B=2's 8** (122-155 s of startup for concurrency a single-user endpoint will not use), and per-request **9.4 tok/s against 20.9** at B=2. The 1.79× rung result stands as a **ctx=32 kernel fact**. **The lever, sized**: `_draft_step` reduces that buffer to `logits[rng, last]` — **8 rows of 248320 = 7.6 MiB against 1444 MiB allocated, 191×** — so reducing the hidden state to those rows *before* the lm_head removes it. **Not** an f16 cast: `kernels_linear.py` declares `Y = T.empty((M, N), "float32")` and `backend.py:499` already records that. Two of my own numbers also died here: the sweep's ctx=32 row reads **60.1** against the A/B's **74.85** at the same config, and multiplying out shows the ticks are **233.8 vs 233.0 ms — 0.3% apart**, so the whole 0.80× is acceptance (`tok/fwd` 14.00 vs 17.52) from `--tokens 32` vs `64` — **two workloads, not a regression**; and the checkpoint vocab is **248320** (under `text_config`), not the 151936 I used, which voided both shape inversions I attempted. [errors/2026-09-03-b8-ceiling-is-the-draft-f32-readout.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-b8-ceiling-is-the-draft-f32-readout.md)

- **verdict** — **B=8 fills the sm70 32 rung and is 1.79× `tilerl serve`'s default.** With `ncols=2` it reads **75.0 / 74.7 tok/s** (two arms 0.40% apart, inside the 1.16% noise floor) at ctx=32 depth 3, against **51.6** with `ncols=1` in the same process: **1.451×**, inside the 1.0-1.5 band committed before the run. **`tok/forward` is 17.52 in all four arms**, so it is pure kernel time, and the `ncols=1` control reproduced to three decimals across two processes. The ncols win turns out **flat across rung occupancy** — **1.498× at B=4** (16 useful of 32 launched) vs **1.451× at B=8** (32 of 32), 3% apart — so `ncols=2` is worth ~1.45-1.50× on the 32 rung *regardless of how much of it is padding*: the win keys on the **compiled rung**, exactly what `_NCOLS_MIN_M` already gates on. I predicted a trend twice and the numbers on the preceding line refuted it both times. **A premise of mine also fell: `tilerl serve` defaults to `--max-batch 2`, not 4** — the 42.7 I twice called "what serving gets today" is a number no code path uses, and B=4 was my own bench parameter. The real baseline is B=2 at **41.8 tok/s** (it fills the 8 rung, but 8 rows sits below the ncols gate, so it gets no `ncols=2` at all), which makes the flip **1.79×** — *larger* than the 1.75× I first published, because the serve default is worse than my bench's B=4. Derived rather than guessed: decode graphs = `buckets ≤ max_batch × widths`, which reproduces both measured precapture lines (12 at B=4, 16 at B=8), so B=2 is 8 and raising the ceiling **doubles the captures** — 122-155 s of startup a single-user endpoint would pay for concurrency it never uses, an independent argument against a blind flip. **Default NOT flipped**: B=8 peaked at 31110 of 32768 MiB and its context ceiling is still unmeasured (stepped sweep running). [wins/2026-09-03-b8-fills-the-32-rung-1.79x-over-serve-default.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-b8-fills-the-32-rung-1.79x-over-serve-default.md)
- **fix** — the sm70 ladder guard in `engine.py` **warned one band too late**: it fired only when `B·W` *exceeded* the top rung and said nothing about landing strictly *between* rungs, where the launch pays for the whole upper rung. At depth 3 that silent band is `max_batch=3..7` — 12/16/20/24/28 rows all launching 32, so **50% of `max_batch=4`'s launch is padding** and, at 7.53 ms per launched row, **120 ms of every tick** goes to rows that do nothing. The warning now names the padding count and the batch size that fills the rung. No shipped default sits in the band (`serve` uses 2, `StepLimits` 8, `generate` 32), so this is a hazard for hand-set values rather than a live bug. Negative control verified: stubbing the new branch to `elif False` fails the test at its assertion. Also fixed **my own bench guard from earlier today**, which asserted `max(rows) >= batch·width` and killed a healthy B=8 run at 28 rows — `verify_lens` trims each request's chain independently, so requiring the full `B·W` needs all B requests to keep every draft in the *same* tick, and **the false-positive rate rises with batch**. It now asserts the invariant that matters (B requests decoded together). 190 tests pass. [wins/2026-09-03-b8-fills-the-32-rung-1.79x-over-serve-default.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-b8-fills-the-32-rung-1.79x-over-serve-default.md)

- **verdict** — **batching is non-monotone on sm70, and a padding row costs 3.3× a useful one.** Same harness, only `--batch` varying (ctx=32, depth 3, `ncols` pinned off): **B=1 32.4 → B=2 41.8 (1.290×) → B=4 28.5 (0.880×, *loses to a single request*) → B=8 51.6 tok/s (1.593×)**. Not acceptance — per-request `tok/forward` is flat at **2.10 / 2.24 / 2.16 / 2.19**; and B=4 re-run inside B=8's process read **28.5 / 35.1 / 8.64**, identical to three decimals across two processes, so the deltas are not drift. The discriminating comparison needs no fit: **B=4 and B=8 launch the same 32 rows**, and doubling the useful rows inside that fixed launch costs **12% more tick time for 2.03× the tokens**. Taking the useful-row coefficient marginally off those two points gives **`tick_ms = 25.6 + 7.53·launched + 2.29·useful`** (held out at L=8 to **3.3%**) — so **a row that exists only to fill the rung costs 3.3× the real work layered on top of it**. Padding is the expensive part; per-request state gathers and KV are cheap once the row is launched. That is the whole story of B=4: `LADDER_WIDTHS` has no rung between 8 and 32, so its 16 useful rows launch as 32, **50% occupancy**. This **withdraws `rows^0.74`, which I committed one tick earlier** — it held B=1/2/4 to 1.3% but assigns one tick per row count, and the two 32-row points differ by 12% (11.3% off at B=8) — plus a "4.1×" padding ratio that compared an average to a marginal, a 1.56× B=2 prediction built from a *width* sweep's ms/row, and a fabricated "14.2 ms of other fixed work" got by subtracting a measured cost from a rejected fit's intercept. **A fit tested only along the direction its inputs move together has not been tested**: every earlier point varied launched and useful rows in lockstep, and B=4 vs B=8 breaks that collinearity by construction. B=8's context ceiling is **not** measured (peak 31110 of 32768 MiB at ctx=32; B=4 already OOMs from ctx≥512). [errors/2026-09-03-batching-is-non-monotone-padding-rows-cost-3x.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-batching-is-non-monotone-padding-rows-cost-3x.md)
- **verdict** — `ncols=2` is a **1.498× win on the speculative verify path**, measured at the batch size serving actually uses. At `max_batch=4` and depth 3 a verify tick submits **16 rows → the 32 rung**, and the arms read **42.7 vs 28.5 tok/s** (confirm arm 42.8, agreeing to **0.23%**, inside the 1.16% noise floor). **`tok/forward` is identical to the hundredth in both arms — 8.64** — so acceptance is untouched and this is pure kernel time: same tokens, same forwards, 33% less time each. The effect is **43× the harness noise floor**, against a 2% threshold committed in the script before launch. Why 1.50 and not the microbench's 1.82: the ladder has no rung between 8 and 32, so 16 real rows are launched as 32 and the kernel does M=32 work for M=16 of use — which puts the number *below* both the M=8 (1.72×) and M=32 (1.82×) microbench points, exactly as that predicts. The three model-level paths are now monotone in **real** rows, which is the mechanism rather than a coincidence: **dense decode M=1 0.951× (loss, gated off) → verify 16 rows 1.498× → prefill M=512 1.520-1.600×**. No code changed; the shipped `_NCOLS_MIN_M = 32` gate already enables this. It replaces the withdrawn "0.995× wash", which was 50 percentage points from the truth because both of its arms ran the 1-column kernel — and **the bring-up cost more than the measurement**: four silent failures (state-pool sizing, a missing `precapture()` for B=4's 12 graphs, an OOM ceiling above ctx=32, and a six-minute hang with the GPU at 0%) stood between `--batch 4` and one usable row. **A perf claim about a row-count-gated path must state the rows it ran** — the gate keys on `B·W`, and naming only the depth hides the half that selects the kernel. [wins/2026-09-03-ncols2-is-1.5x-on-the-verify-path.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-ncols2-is-1.5x-on-the-verify-path.md)


- **withdrawal** — the spec-decode `ncols=2` measurement **never ran the kernel it claimed to test**, so #36's "wash" is withdrawn. `bench_ctx_decode.py` submits **one** request and `max_batch=4` is only a ceiling, so a depth-3 verify tick was **B·W = 1×4 = 4 rows on the 4 rung** — below `_NCOLS_MIN_M=32`, which means `TILERL_NCOLS=2` and `=1` compiled and ran the **identical 1-column kernel**. The flag was read and the dispatch consulted it; the row count never reached the threshold that makes it matter. **The tell was in the numbers I published**: 0.995/1.000/0.988/0.996/0.998× across a 128× context sweep is tighter than two different kernels track, and I recorded it as "worst 1.2%, inside the threshold" — reading a null result as a passed test. Second time today an A/B compared a kernel against itself; the first was `ncols` landing positionally on `abl`, and the keyword-only fix that killed that class for good is useless here, because this one routes through **data shape** rather than argument order. Blast radius, checked: #36's ratio is void (the nc1 column survives as a valid B=1 depth-3 baseline, and it is the one the depth comparisons reuse); the cost line `0.670 + 0.5265·W` **survives with its axis corrected** — it is a B=1 fit where rows *equal* W (2/4/8 rows on the 2/4/8 rungs, no rounding), so its W is a **chain width, not a launched-row count**, which is how this was found (pricing the ladder with it gave a trim choosing W=2 at every acceptance including 0.95, since at B=4 widths 3 and 4 both launch 32 rows and cannot cost 2.78 and 4.75); the rung gate's threshold is **unaffected**, coming from M=1 (−4.9%) and M=512 (1.52×), both real. Fixed with `--batch N` on the bench and a test that spies on `_run_forward` and asserts the widest pure-decode tick exceeds 4 rows with 4 concurrent requests — **negative control verified**: at B=1 the widest tick is exactly 4 rows and the assertion fails. **An A/B whose arms agree to a fraction of a percent has probably not been run**, and **a benchmark's batch size is part of the kernel it selects**. [errors/2026-09-03-the-spec-ncols-ab-ran-at-b1.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-the-spec-ncols-ab-ran-at-b1.md)


- **reject** — repricing `verify_lens` for sm70 is **the wrong fix, and no constants changed**. Last tick's finding stands (H20's 211.0/0.53 ms against a measured 15.9/12.5) but my diagnosis of it was wrong in three ways that 12 lines of `_run_forward` settle without a GPU. The tick pays for the **widest chain rounded to a ladder rung**, not for total rows: `engine.py:684` pads every chain to `max(len)`, so at the shipped `max_batch=4` a trim from W=4 to W=3 submits **12 rows → the same 32-row rung → identical cost**, which is the repriced trim's entire visible effect at high acceptance. The trim also optimizes the wrong quantity — its denominator is total rows admitted while the bill is `max(keep)+1` — and where the reprice *does* bite it bites backwards: the measured price puts the keep≤1 boundary at acceptance **p≈0.92** against a recorded **84.4%**, so it would force W≤2 at exactly the contexts where depth 3 earns **1.157× (512)** and **1.228× (1024)** vs depth 1's 1.133×/1.112×. Kept separate and intact: the cost line itself picks the winning depth **5/5 across contexts with 0.1-2.5% error on the speedup** — it is sound for choosing W against a rung table off-line, and only the plan to inject it into a function whose cost is continuous in a different variable died. The real fix, if the acceptance distribution ever justifies it, is a **rung-aware** trim (enumerate `LADDER_WIDTHS`, price each with the measured line) — a different function, not different constants. `spec.py` gains the shape mismatch in its docstring and a `__main__` assertion that W=3 and W=4 share a rung at B=4, so the reprice cannot be re-attempted silently. **Read the consumer before pricing the producer**: matching algebraic forms are what made the substitution look safe, and are not matching models. And **a fix whose effect you cannot state in rows, tokens or milliseconds is not a fix yet** — "the constants are 13× off" was enough to file a task and not enough to change a line. [errors/2026-09-03-repricing-verify-lens-was-the-wrong-fix.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-repricing-verify-lens-was-the-wrong-fix.md)


- **verdict + correction** — a verify tick costs **`0.670 + 0.5265·W` dense ticks**, accepted out-of-sample, and the "constant 2.75" I published last tick was that line evaluated at one width. Five contexts at depth 3 gave 2.72-2.82 (1.8% spread) and three independent derivations agreed, so I called it a property of speculation; the quantity in the *mechanism* — the verify width W — I never varied. Measured at W=2 (depth 1) it is **1.723 ±0.4% over a 128× context range**, not 2.75. **Committed before the confirming run**: the two-point fit predicts **4.88** at W=8, accept within ±15%. Measured mean of four usable contexts **4.75 (2.7% off)**, every point inside the band — at twice the fitted span's extrapolation distance. A fifth row printed `UNWARMED` (the harness flags a third pass beating its second by >2×) and is excluded; **the flag fired inside the arm I was validating**, where an optimistic number would have read as confirmation. Per-context spread at W=8 is 13% against W=2's 0.4%, with **no trend** — and that nearly went in as one: three of four rows arrived in descending order (4.881/4.809/4.355), I read a decline, and I had a mechanism written for it before ctx=4096 came back the highest of the four at 4.941. **A three-point monotone sequence is not a trend**; nothing was published only because the run finished first, which is luck rather than method. The slope being **below 1.0** is why speculation can pay at all: rows share the weight stream, so a marginal verify row costs just over half a dense tick. The line reproduces the four-month-old depth staircase with no free parameters — depth 4 wants W=5, the ladder has no rung there, so it launches 8 rows and must beat **4.88** tok/forward instead of 2.78 — and only depths **1/3/7/31** launch the rows they use. **Flat in the variable you swept is not constant**: name the quantity the mechanism says it depends on and vary *that*. [wins/2026-09-03-verify-tick-cost-is-a-line-in-width.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-verify-tick-cost-is-a-line-in-width.md)

- **finding** — the shipped `spec_depth=3` is the **worst of three options at short context**: at ctx=32 it measures **38.0 tok/s against dense's 43.1 (0.882×, a 12% loss)** while **depth 1 is 43.5 (1.009×)**. Depth 1 was never measured before; it never loses at any context (worst reading 1.009×) and wins outright at 32 and 2048, while depth 3 is clearly best only at 512-1024 (1.157×, 1.228×). Depth 7 pays **only** at 1024 (1.166×) and never beats depth 3 — 0.578× at 32 and 0.660× at 4096 — so 8 rows is past this draft's useful width and no wider rung is worth trying. The cause is **dense improving, not spec degrading**: dense decode gains 34% from 4096 to 32 (39.1 → 43.1) as its KV read shrinks, while a verify tick's cost barely moves — so **every dense win erodes speculation's margin and moves the crossover**, including the `ncols=2` prefill work shipped the same day. Default not flipped: the crossover sits between 32 and 512 and only short-prompt requests are in the loss region, so the serving mix decides whether the branch is worth it. [wins/2026-09-03-verify-tick-cost-is-a-line-in-width.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-verify-tick-cost-is-a-line-in-width.md)

- **finding** — `verify_lens` prices the draft trim with **H20 constants on sm70, 13× and 24× off** (211.0/0.53 ms against a measured 15.9/12.5), and the comment calling it "mispriced-but-inert" is wrong on both clauses: `engine.py:974` calls it from `_draft_chains` on **every** spec tick — capture replays the *verify*, while the trim decides what enters it. Substituting the measured pair changes the trim's decision at every acceptance level (batch of 4, depth 3): high 0.95/0.90/0.85 keeps **3 vs 2**, mid 0.8/0.6/0.4 keeps **3 vs 1**, low 0.5/0.25/0.1 keeps **2 vs 0**. **Superseded the same day**: those decision changes are mostly free of charge, because the tick's cost is set by the widest chain rounded to a ladder rung and W=3 launches the same 32 rows as W=4 at `max_batch=4` — the reprice is rejected and no constants changed, see the entry above. Constants left in place with the shape mismatch annotated beside them. [errors/2026-09-03-repricing-verify-lens-was-the-wrong-fix.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-repricing-verify-lens-was-the-wrong-fix.md)


- **correction + verdict** — the `ncols=2` gate **leaves speculative decode ON**, which the entry below stated three times it did not, and measured there it is a **wash**. The sm70 ladder is `1/2/4/8/32` with **no rung between 8 and 32**, so `_sm70_chunks` rounds any M in 9..31 *up* to 32 — and the engine's defaults (`max_batch=4`, `spec_depth=3` → W=4) submit **B·W=16 rows → rung 32 → `ncols=2` on**. The gate exempts dense decode only. Measured on the verify path, threshold 2% committed in the script first: **0.995 / 1.000 / 0.988 / 0.996 / 0.998×** at 32/512/1024/2048/4096, worst **1.2%** — inside the threshold, so `_NCOLS_MIN_M` stays a rung threshold and **no code changed**. Worth the tick anyway, because spec was a third unmeasured path whose row count sits *between* the two measured regimes and the mechanism argument cuts both ways there: 16 rows share a weight stream (arithmetic per byte up, like prefill) but the grid still halves onto shapes that lost 4.9% at M=1. The three paths now read monotone in rows — **prefill M=512 1.52-1.60× win, spec M=16 0.995× wash, dense M=1 0.951× loss** — which is the mechanism, not a coincidence. Fixed the false clause in `backend.py`, the error entry and the CHANGELOG, and closed the test's blind spot: the rung loop probed `(1,2,4,8,32)`, i.e. **every ladder-exact width and therefore precisely not the 9..31 interval where rounding happens**; it now probes 9/12/16/24/31 (ON), 40 (`[True,False]`) and 512 (all ON), with two negative controls verified. **A test that only samples the exact boundaries cannot find a rounding bug**, and **read the function, not your last sentence about it** — this was one `_sm70_chunks(16)` call from being checked. [errors/2026-09-03-the-ncols-gate-left-spec-decode-on.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-the-ncols-gate-left-spec-decode-on.md)

- **error + default flip** — `ncols=2` **cost 4.9% of dense decode**, and is now gated to the top rung. It shipped as a default flip on the *whole* sm70 ladder on the strength of a prefill A/B; decode was never run. Measured, arms nc2/nc1/nc2: **39.1 → 37.2 tok/s at 4096**, a uniform 0.946-0.951× at every context, with the confirm arm reproducing arm 1 to the decimal at all five points. **The mechanism inverts below the top rung**: `ncols=2` wins by raising HFMA2 per LDG, which only pays where the kernel is compute-bound — at M=1 the GEMV is bandwidth-bound (84% of its byte roofline), so there is no arithmetic to win and only the halved grid remains (`ceildiv(N/2, n_partition)`), landing on shapes task #21 had already recorded at **5-33% of peak**: down/attn-o falls to **8.0 blocks/SM**. The microbench could not have caught it — M=1 read **1.05× against its own ±4% noise floor**, i.e. inside the error bar, and six shapes in isolation cannot show grid starvation among 144 competing launches. Fix is one expression on the rung the ladder already carries: `nc = nc2 if Mk >= 32 else 1`, so prefill's sixteen 32-row chunks keep `ncols=2` while dense decode gets the 1-column kernel (a verify tick's 16 rows round up to the 32 rung and keep it — see the correction above). Verified both paths: decode **39.1 tok/s, 1.000× of control** at 4096; prefill **8.91 → 5.86 ms/token (1.52×)**, 7.88 → 4.91 (1.60×) at 512, compared within one harness because `bench_prefill.py` and `ab_prefill_ncols.py` disagree 1-3% on identical code. Contract test grew a fifth assertion walking `_sm70_chunks` for every rung decode and verify take (negative control verified) — the failure mode is silent, costing throughput with nothing raising. **A default flip is a claim about every path, so it needs a number from every path**, and **a reading inside its own noise floor is not a green light**. [errors/2026-09-03-ncols2-cost-5-percent-of-decode.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-ncols2-cost-5-percent-of-decode.md)

- **reject** — prefill attention is **deferred, and last tick's "next lever" is withdrawn**. The mechanism predicted from reading the kernel is confirmed: `S` is a grid dim, so every query row is its own block re-reading the whole K/V window, and **µs/row goes 216.5 → 138.7 from S=1 to S=512 (0.64×, threshold 1.25× committed in the script before the run)** — batching 512 rows recovers only **1.56× of a possible ~512×** sharing, all incidental L2 reuse. Two instruments agree it is the shipped path: 71.0 ms at S=512 over a 4096 window halves to 35.5 ms at prefill's ~2048 average, against 33.7 ms measured in-model (**1.05×**). But the ceiling kills it: on the profiled mix (GEMV 16327 ms, attention 4378, rest 1677) attention at **infinite** speed is worth **1.243×** of prefill — 2× buys 1.108×, 4× buys 1.172× — against **1.57× for a further 2× on the GEMV**. A Q-tiled flash kernel means a new parity gate, an online-softmax rewrite across a query tile, and SMEM budgeting on 96 KB/SM, for at most 1.243×. Also established without writing anything: the factory knobs are already near-optimal — nine `(KVSPLIT, block_N)` cells, monotone in both, shipped (32,16) within 17% of the best, and `block_N=64` 6× worse because the staged `Kf/Vf` fragments are 64 KB. Threads reconfirm the 2026-09-01 redundancy fix held (248/158/154/302 µs at 32/64/128/256t). **Price the ceiling before measuring the mechanism** — both numbers came from a profile I already had, and **distance from peak is not prize size**: a term at 19.6% of the tick cannot pay more than 1.243× however badly it is written. [errors/2026-09-03-attention-ceiling-is-1.24x-priority-withdrawn.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-attention-ceiling-is-1.24x-priority-withdrawn.md)

- **verdict** — prefill's growing share **is** attention, and it runs at **0.75 TFLOPS**. The Amdahl inversion in the `ncols=2` entry claimed the GEMV share falls with context and that what grows is attention; a two-context profile confirms the identity and corrects one number. **Attention is the only class that is not flat per token: 0.211 → 1.069 ms/token from 512 to 4096 (5.07×), while GEMV, GDN, elementwise, rmsnorm and "other" all reproduce to within 0.1%** — so the six-class flatness doubles as evidence the instrument measures what it claims. Attention's share of GPU goes **4.6% → 19.6%**. Reconstructing the pre-fix mix (scale the measured GEMV back by 1.82×) gives shares 0.921/0.831 and predicted gains 1.710×/1.598× against the measured 1.694×/1.585× — the inversion was sound to 1%, but the "18% non-GEMV" it quoted was the *pre*-fix figure; **post-fix the remainder at 4096 is 27.1%**, so further GEMV work caps at 1.57× (2×), 2.21× (4×), 3.70× (∞). **The instrument was broken first and would have refuted a true claim**: `prof_prefill_budget.py` windowed one `e.step()`, which above the 512-token chunk budget is chunk *one* — so `--ctx 512` and `--ctx 4096` profiled byte-identical work and no context dependence could appear; `--all` now spans every chunk. What this decides: attention is one kernel at **4310.9 ms of a 22382 ms prefill**, 3.3 TFLOP in 4378 ms = **42× off the 31.4 TFLOPS scalar peak**, while re-reading 6912 GiB of f32 K+V at 1695 GB/s — so neither the FLOP nor the HBM roofline binds, and the cost is the kernel's shape (one query row per thread, `block_N=16` walked serially, f32 on a card with no packed-f32 FMA). **An inversion gives you a magnitude, never an identity.** [wins/2026-09-03-prefill-attention-is-what-grows.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-prefill-attention-is-what-grows.md)

- **default flip** — `ncols=2` **ships**: sm70 prefill is **8.66 → 5.47 ms/token at 4096 (1.59×)**, 8.17 → 4.97 at 2048 (1.64×), 7.81 → 4.61 at 512 (1.69×), and **TTFT at 4096 falls 35.5 → 22.4 s**. Greedy text is byte-identical to `ncols=1` on real prompts at M=1 and at 600 tokens (M>8), which is the check that matters when the failure mode is corrupting half the output columns. Measured in one process with one set of weights, arms ordered nc2/nc1/nc2 — the two nc2 readings agreed to 1.000×, and the nc1 arm reproduced the recorded 7.89/8.33/8.92 baseline to 1-3%, so it is a control and not an unknown. **Inverting Amdahl prices the rest of prefill with no profiler**: a 1.82× kernel yielding 1.694/1.644/1.585× implies a GEMV share of **0.91 / 0.87 / 0.82** at 512/2048/4096 — the share *falls* with context, so what grows is attention, the one quadratic part (both halves since confirmed by profile, and the remainder corrected to 27.1%). [wins/2026-09-03-ncols2-raises-loads-per-fma.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-ncols2-raises-loads-per-fma.md)

- **error** — the `ncols=2` prefill A/B was **measuring `abl`, not `ncols`**, and both arms were kernels that return wrong numbers by design. The dispatch passed the flag positionally as `(Mk, 4, True, sh, 0, nc)` against a factory whose 6th parameter is `abl`, so `TILERL_NCOLS=1` ran **X_REUSE** — whose loop-invariant address lets ptxas delete 85% of the loads — and read **2.35 ms/token @4096, a fake 3.8× "win"**; `TILERL_NCOLS=2` ran **NO_SCALE**, measured 0.92× and therefore nearly free, so it sat on the recorded baseline at 9.35. Both readings were internally consistent and entirely fictional, and the table read as "ncols=2 is a 4× regression" — the exact opposite of the microbench. What caught it was not the reversal but the magnitude against a **recorded** number: `TILERL_NCOLS=1` *is* the shipped path by construction, and the shipped path is 8.92; a flag cannot make the code it disables 3.8× faster than that code. A judge run of the unmodified HEAD backend reproduced **7.88 / 8.03 / 8.33 / 8.91** against the recorded 7.89 / 8.03 / 8.33 / 8.92 (0.1%), clearing the instrument. The same output carried a second tell I nearly missed: **first-token id was 0 in every arm at every context**, which greedy argmax over a real 27B does not do — and it read 0 for the *correct* kernels too, so that signal was empty, not merely ignored: synthetic ids make no usable parity check, which is why `scripts/parity_ncols.py` now decodes real text instead. Fixed at the depth where the bug class dies: `min_blocks`/`abl`/`ncols` are **keyword-only**, `Backend._kernel` takes `**kw` and keys its cache on it, and the dispatch names `ncols=nc`; `abl=6` is deleted so the harness reaches NCOLS2 the same way serving does (a stale `abl=6` would have silently compiled the *base* kernel and reported 1.00×). **A flag that makes code deliberately wrong must not be reachable by position**, and **the arm that is supposed to be current behavior is a control — price it against its recorded number before reading the delta**. [errors/2026-09-03-the-ab-measured-abl-not-ncols.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-the-ab-measured-abl-not-ncols.md)

- **verdict** — `ncols=2` is **1.82× at M=32** (accept threshold ≥1.25×, committed before the run), the first win in this family after eight rejections, and it confirms what binds. One thread computes two output columns from one X load, so the same `xw[8]` feeds two weight sets and 16 FMAs: **HFMA2 per LDG 3.53 → 6.06**, a 1.72× ratio change against a measured 1.72× at M=8 and 1.82× at M=32 — **the speedup tracks the ratio**, which is the check SMEM taught (it did exactly what it promised, removing 86% of loads, and lost anyway). Registers 255/24 B-spill → **254/zero spills**: doubling the arithmetic per thread made it cheaper per register, not dearer. M=1 is 1.05×, inside its ±4% noise floor, so no regression. **When every candidate fails, look for what they all preserved** — eight mechanisms were ranked by which quantity they reduced when the question was which quantity binds, and the answer (loads per FMA) was visible in any two cubins side by side. **A failed experiment's byproducts are evidence**: SMEM's rejected 127-register/zero-spill kernel is what made `ncols` affordable after it was set aside as contraindicated at 255. Wired into the dispatch behind `Np == N and N % 2 == 0` — the kernel derives `half = N//2` from the *padded* `Np`, so a padded shape would pair a real column with a pad column and write garbage into `Y[:, N/2:]` that the `[:Mr,:N]` slice keeps — with `tests/test_ncols_contract.py` checking the guard's own source (both negative controls verified). **The full-model prefill number is still `pending-remote`**; the first attempt measured the wrong kernel entirely (see the error entry above). Prize if it lands: prefill **8.92 → 5.50 ms/token, TTFT 36.5 → 22.5 s**. [wins/2026-09-03-ncols2-raises-loads-per-fma.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-ncols2-raises-loads-per-fma.md)

- **reject** — SMEM staging measures **0.67× (33% SLOWER)** at M=32 against a ≥1.30× threshold committed first, and the cubin explains the whole six-tick search. It did what it promised: `X[0, base]` carries no `ni`, so all four `n_partition` groups load the same X, and staging cut **LDG 363 → 51 (86% gone)**, registers **255 → 127 with zero spills**. Still slower, because ptxas vectorized the staged words into two `LDS.128` — **swapping LDG for LDS one-for-one** and leaving the per-row ratio at **1 load : 4 HFMA2**, then adding the stage (8 LDG + 16 STS + 2 barriers) on top. **The binding quantity is loads per FMA**, and every failed candidate preserved it: reorder (PIPELINE 0.99×), memory space (SMEM 0.67×), block shape (`n_partition`), accumulator chain, register budget (`min_blocks` 1.00×). The only variant that ever beat the kernel is X_REUSE (8.77×), which breaks the ratio by *deleting* the loads — not a fix. **Read the ratio, not the count**: "removes 86% of global loads" is true and irrelevant. Two implementation facts were fixed *before* measuring — 128 KB of staged tile against Volta's 96 KB/SM (so `g` moved to tilelang, `G=1`), and a first version staging with `if ni == 0` that cuts traffic 4× but issues **32 serial LDG per lane** where spreading rows issues 8, with one block/SM and nothing to overlap; **a candidate's own flaw impersonates "this direction does not work"**, so that pod run was killed by fd-verified pid rather than allowed to vote. What the rejection *establishes*: a 127-register kernel with no spills reopens the `ncols` direction (one thread over several output columns), which was set aside as unaffordable at 255 registers and is the only remaining way to raise the ratio. [errors/2026-09-03-smem-staging-is-slower-loads-per-fma-binds.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-smem-staging-is-slower-loads-per-fma-binds.md)

- **reject** — prefetching X: **PIPELINE 0.99× at M=32**, threshold ≥1.15× committed in the script first, bit-exact as a reorder must be. This is the measurement that splits `X_REUSE`'s 8.80×, which had changed three things at once (traffic, latency, and 85% of the LDG instructions — a loop-invariant address is hoistable). `abl=4` issues row m+1's loads before row m's FMA block: same addresses, same 363 LDG, only the schedule differs. It recovers **0.0-1.8% of the headroom, ~0% at M=32**. **Latency hiding is the cheapest thing a scheduler can do; when it does nothing, the constraint is throughput** — so the cost is X's traffic plus the issue slots its 310 extra LDGs occupy, and reordering cannot touch either. That makes SMEM staging the right *shape* of fix (it removes global loads rather than rescheduling them), with an honest ceiling: staging swaps 310 LDG for LDS on the same 128 B/cycle port, so it wins on traffic and L1/L2 pressure, not on issue slots. The specific prize the numbers now favour: `n_partition=4` has four column-groups per block each loading **the same X**, which one SMEM stage collapses. Also recorded: X_REUSE at M=1 read 0.97× and 1.01× on two runs of the same kernel, so **±4% is the M=1 noise floor** and no sub-1.05× reading there is signal; M=8 and M=32 reproduce to 2% and 0.6%. [errors/2026-09-03-prefetching-x-buys-nothing.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-prefetching-x-buys-nothing.md)

- **verdict** — **X dominates the sm70 GEMV at M=32**, located by ablation after five A/Bs excluded everything else. Keep the instruction and load counts identical, remove one suspect's *cost*: `X_REUSE` (every row reads row 0's X, so an L1 hit after the first) is **8.75× at M=32**, 3.05× at M=8, 0.97× at M=1 — only a per-row cost scales like that. `NO_SCALE` is 0.92× and `NO_DECODE` 0.98×, so the per-tile scale tail — **28.5% of the instruction stream** by SASS count — and the fp4 dequant are both **free**, entirely hidden behind X. That also retires "reduce instructions per flop", which the previous entry derived as the only remaining lever. **This reverses two of my own rejections**: SMEM staging was dropped because a two-K sweep found no L1-capacity knee, but the ablation says its target was right all along — the *prediction* was false, not the suspicion; and "L1 bandwidth is not binding" rested on X reading at 35% of the port, which is a rate, not a limit (loads can dominate while running below peak — that is what latency-bound means). The mechanism is **not established**: the capacity model (knee at `M=131072/(2K)`) and a reuse-distance model (`n_partition·K/2`, M-independent — which is why both knee curves flattened together) are each refuted by their own control, K=1280 being 4× deeper in L1 and slower at every M. Not publishing a third story. **Ablate before modelling** — one ablation ranked the candidates by cost and the ranking was nothing like the guesses. **Correction, same day:** the 8.75× is an upper bound on *everything* X-related, not X's loads alone — a loop-invariant address is hoistable and `nvdisasm` shows **LDG 363 → 53**, so 85% of the loads were deleted rather than cached, and the delta prices the issue slots too. **Check that an ablation removed the cost and not the instructions** — one `nvdisasm | grep -c`, which I skipped before publishing. `abl=4` (PIPELINE, same 363 LDG, correct output) is the honest traffic-vs-latency split. [wins/2026-09-03-x-dominates-the-gemv-at-m32.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-x-dominates-the-gemv-at-m32.md)

- **feature** — the narrow-rmsnorm invariant is a gate instead of a fact someone remembers. `narrow=True` (f16 output for the fp4 GEMV, +4.0-4.3% dense decode) is sound only where every consumer is a linear; rope, attention and the GDN scan are f32, and a f32→f16→f32 round trip there drops 13 mantissa bits. **Nothing numeric can catch that** — `rope` calls `_f32()` defensively, which restores the dtype and not the bits, and is why the arch-wide version shipped through a green suite. `tests/test_narrow_norm.py` parses `model.py` and asserts the dataflow: every `narrow=True` output reaches nothing but `_linear`/`_base_linear`/`_add_via`, q_norm/k_norm are never narrowed, `backend.rmsnorm` gates on `gemv_io != float32` rather than on the flag (or sm90 and cpu would narrow too), and `reference.rmsnorm` ignores the flag so the CPU twin stays a real parity target. Two negative controls run: a `backend.rope(h, …)` added after a narrowed norm fails naming `['rope']`, and narrowing q_norm fails at `model.py:209`. 0.9 s, source-only, no card. 182 passed. [wins/2026-09-03-narrow-norm-invariant-is-structural.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-03-narrow-norm-invariant-is-structural.md)

- **reject** — occupancy as the sm70 GEMV's cap. `nvcc -Xptxas=-v` on tilelang's cached kernel (ncu is denied on this pod, this is not) reports **255 registers** — the per-thread max — so 255 × 128 threads = **one block per SM = 4 of Volta's 64 warps, 6.25% occupancy**, with an instruction mix of 2936 total / **1280 HFMA2** = 2.29 per FMA → issue ceiling **13.6 TFLOPS**. `T.annotate_min_blocks_per_sm(4)` halves registers to 128 (4 blocks, 16 warps) at 180 B of spill instead of 24. Threshold ≥1.15× committed first; measured **1.00× at M=32**, 0.84× at M=8, 1.00× at M=1, bit-identical. 4× the warps moving nothing refutes the diagnosis, not the register count. The per-shape M=8 split (N=5120 loses 0.55-0.63×, N≥14336 gains 1.04-1.06×) is **not** grid starvation as I first read it — every shape queues ≥16 blocks/SM; it is a fixed spill cost against kernel duration. **Five mechanisms are now excluded by measurement** (FMA chain, `n_partition`, L1 capacity, L1 bandwidth, occupancy) and M=32 sits at 17.6% of FMA peak, 35% of the L1 ceiling, 40% of the issue ceiling — the next attempt needs a different *instrument*, not another hypothesis. **Do not write the win entry before the A/B returns**: I had published this as `wins/gemv-is-occupancy-capped` while the measurement ran, and deleted it rather than leave two entries contradicting each other. `min_blocks` stays as a factory flag defaulting to 0 (shipped behaviour unchanged); `scripts/sass_gemv_mix.py` is the new static instrument. [errors/2026-09-03-occupancy-is-not-the-gemv-cap.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-occupancy-is-not-the-gemv-cap.md)

- **reject** — SMEM staging for the sm70 M-path GEMV, killed before a kernel was written, and with it the "4.1× off the FLOP floor" this branch has quoted three times. Its mechanism (X[32,5120] = 328 KB spills the 128 KB L1) predicts a knee at `M = 131072/(2K)` — **M≈12.8 at K=5120, M≈51.2 at K=1280** — and **a capacity knee moves with K while a schedule knee does not**. Sweeping M at both K values with the shipped kernel: **both flatten at M≈12-24**, and K=1280 saturates by M=24 with X at 160 KB even at M=64. No knee, no capacity limit, nothing written. The same arithmetic corrects the target: the extern loads 2×`v4.u32` = **32 B of X** and issues 8 `fma.rn.f16x2` = **32 flops** (kernels_linear.py:638-651), so the kernel is **1 flop per X byte structurally**, X traffic is `N·M·K·2` (every output column re-reads all of X), and the L1 port (15.7 TB/s) caps it at **15.7 TFLOPS = 50% of the 31.3 FMA peak** with perfect hits. **The real gap is ~2×, not 4.1×** — three entries measured against a peak this kernel cannot reach. L1 bandwidth is not binding either (M=32 reads X at 5.51 TB/s = 35% of the port), so FMA issue, `n_partition`, L1 capacity and L1 bandwidth are all now excluded, and the only lever the equation leaves is **raising flops per X byte**: one thread over `ncols` output columns cuts X traffic `ncols`× and moves the ceiling to 62.8 TFLOPS at 4, with the register cost on `d0/d1` rather than on X. First run of the script reported **5687 TFLOPS, 182× V100's peak** — `bk.timeit` returns milliseconds and I divided as microseconds; no derived value from that run was reported. `scripts/ab_gemv_l1_knee.py`. [errors/2026-09-03-smem-staging-rejected-one-flop-per-x-byte.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-03-smem-staging-rejected-one-flop-per-x-byte.md)

## 2026-09-02

- **phase exit** — stage 4(b), judge verdicts reach GRPO: tests decide first and the judge only breaks ties inside the all-pass or all-fail subgroup, so no judged ordering lifts a failure above a pass (disjoint bands, 0.6–1.0 and 0.0–0.4). Only the ORDER is used — Copeland win counts, not Elo, because pairwise LLM verdicts are not guaranteed transitive and a cycle must read as "no signal" rather than an invented ranking. A judge that cannot separate a group injects zero advantage, which is the property that stops a useless judge from looking like learning. Gate `tests/test_judge.py` (7 tests, incl. the non-transitive and always-tie cases).
- **verdict** — the GDN verify scatter is a **604 MB double write**, and it is deferred. `record_shapes=True` resolves the largest op as `_index_put_impl_` self `[4,4,48,128,128]` ← value `[4,48,128,128]`, 48 launches / **2.154 ms** — `state_scatter`'s `step_states[slots, layer, :ks] = new_state` at 12.58 MB per layer × 48 layers. Read+write 1.21 GB = 1.34 ms at 900 GB/s, so **1.6× off its own bytes: bandwidth, not launch overhead**. The previous entry's "10.5× off bytes" was an average over five ops spanning **16 B to 12.58 MB** — resolved by shape, one is a real memcpy and another (`parity[slots]`, 96 launches for a 16-byte read) is real launch overhead, and **those two have opposite fixes**. The write is also redundant: `_gdn_chunk_fused` passes `step_states` in as an operand and the kernel writes it (kernels_gdn.py:380), then `state_scatter` copies that scratch into the pool. Fix has a precedent one function away — `gdn_decode_fused` takes `pool.states` + `slots_i` + `int(layer)` and writes in place; the same operands on `gdn_chunk_fused` delete the scatter *and* the 0.663 ms gather. **Not taken**: 2.44 ms of a 66.46 ms tick is **1.038×** (whole-region ceiling 1.106×) for a kernel-ABI change across three files plus a parity gate plus a CPU-twin conditional, while `linear_fp4_gemv_sm70_m` is **41.49 ms = 62%** of the same tick. **A per-launch cost is meaningless across ops of different sizes.** [errors/2026-09-02-gdn-verify-scatter-is-a-double-write.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-02-gdn-verify-scatter-is-a-double-write.md)

- **verdict** — the ~1400 torch elementwise launches in a spec tick are the **trunk's GDN state path**, not the draft: region attribution gives `trunk.gdn` **792 launches / 6.40 ms** of 8.80 total torch ms, and the whole draft head **8 launches / 0.02 ms**. Inside GDN the ops name themselves — `_index_put_impl_` 96/2.45 ms and `aten::index` 144/1.13 ms are exactly `state_gather`'s 3 fancy-index gathers plus `state_scatter`'s 2 index_puts (reference.py:857-882) × 48 layers, 240 launches / 3.57 ms at **10.5× their own byte cost** (302 MB = 0.34 ms at 900 GB/s). Negative control `--depth 0`: `trunk.gdn` 58/0.12 ms with both index ops **absent**, because `gdn_decode_fused` updates the pool in place at T=1. This **withdraws** the differencing claim ("one draft forward adds 955 launches / 6.91 ms, 33× first-vs-later") — `spec d1` and `dense` differ in two things, the draft *and* 48 GDN layers switching off the fused decode path, and everything on the right of that plus sign landed in the column labelled "draft". **A difference attributes to one thing only if the two configurations differ in one thing.** Method: six `record_function` wrappers on `Model`'s own methods; the profiler nests CUDA events under the launching CPU range and its `name` is the aten op — the attribution `with_stack` cannot give on this build. `scripts/prof_region_attrib.py`. [errors/2026-09-02-differencing-attributed-the-trunk-to-the-draft.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-02-differencing-attributed-the-trunk-to-the-draft.md)

- **fix** — `_quantize_draft` was not idempotent: `build_engine` writes its output back into `draft.params` in place (DraftHead.layers is a Model over that dict), so a second engine over the same draft packed the already-packed `fc.wq` into `fc.wq.wq` and the draft forward raised `KeyError: 'fc'`. One engine per process is the shipped path, so nothing hit it until a profiler compared three configurations; a train loop rebuilding an engine per phase or recapture-after-update would. **An in-place transform of shared state is a fixed point by construction or a bug waiting for a second call** — decided in the function, not by how often the caller runs. Gate `test_quantize_draft_is_idempotent`, negative control fails naming `fc.wq.wq`. No effect on the single-engine path. [errors/2026-09-02-quantize-draft-not-idempotent.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-02-quantize-draft-not-idempotent.md)

- **reject** — capturing `_draft_step` in the decode graph, and with it the **1.18× (50.3 → 59.5 tok/s)** this branch published twice: per-kernel attribution puts **58.5 ms of GPU-busy in a 66.46 ms depth-3 tick — 88% GPU-bound**, so removing every host launch caps at 1.14×, and 71% of that GPU time is one kernel (`linear_fp4_gemv_sm70_m`, 332 launches × 125 µs = 41.49 ms) which a graph replays at the same speed. The estimate came from a byte roofline (954 MB → 1.06 ms vs 5.53 measured = "5.2× overhead") plus transferring the trunk's 2.66× capture win by analogy — but a draft forward's ~9 GEMV launches are already ~1.12 ms of GPU time at 125 µs each, so **the byte floor was a factor of 5 below the real one**: the GEMV at M=1 is launch-shaped, not byte-shaped, and there was never host overhead in that gap. Reaching any of the 1.14× would need a bucketed-shape rewrite of the draft path (variable `w`/`nb`, per-tick allocation, data-dependent `live`, a `.tolist()`). Also fixed: `prof_draft_kernels.py` took `--source` and never used it, so it fetched the placeholder hub id — `_build_model` now takes `source=` (three scripts have hit this). The profiler's own wall reads 121.5 ms/tick against a real 66.46, so its wall and host share are instrument artifacts and are not quoted. [errors/2026-09-02-capturing-the-draft-is-rejected.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-02-capturing-the-draft-is-rejected.md)

- **phase exit** — dense decode **+4.0-4.3%** at every context (4096 37.6 → 39.1 tok/s, 32 41.6 → 43.4): the sm70 GEMV reads X in f16 and the dispatch was casting it at every launch, over bytes `rmsnorm_apply`/`silu_mul` had just written in f32. Both kernels already had a bf16 twin for sm90, so the out dtype becomes a factory arg and the producers write f16 directly — 193 of the 305 casts a dense token pays, predicted at 3.7% (1.64 ms × 193/305 over a 27.8 ms token) before the bench read 4.0-4.3%. The first version replaced `rmsnorm_apply` outright and silently made `q_norm`/`k_norm` **worse than untouched**: their consumers are rope and attention, both f32, so it was a f32→f16→f32 round trip dropping 13 mantissa bits and ADDING 32 casts — invisible to every gate (parity 4.93e-04 unchanged, bench up, suite green) because `rope` defensively calls `_f32()`. Scoping it to the four sites that feed a linear (`narrow=True`, off by default, separate registry key) measured faster too. **Narrowing a dtype is a property of the edge, not the op.** Remaining: the 112 launches fed by attention and the GDN scan, ~2.2% on the same arithmetic. Gates: compile 34/34, `parity_sm70_gemv_m` 4.93e-04, 177 CPU tests. [wins/2026-09-02-elementwise-writes-the-gemv-dtype.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-02-elementwise-writes-the-gemv-dtype.md)
- **reject** — the post-precapture "ramp" (13.6 → 25.5 tok/s over four requests) and its fp4-JIT suspect: `grep -c "begins to compile"` on a serve log already sitting on the pod returns **3**, all timestamped inside precapture, none during requests. Decode-only with equal token counts reads 32.9 / 31.8 / 24.4 / 31.2 / 39.4 ms/token — request 0 already at 30.4 tok/s, ±23% spread in no order, reserved memory moving 6 MiB and 0 allocator retries. It was prompt-dependent acceptance (tok/forward 1.6-3.8), and the HTTP curve was prefill first-use inside the window again. **Grep the log before writing the profiler**, and four points are not a trend. [errors/2026-09-02-the-ramp-was-prompt-variance.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-02-the-ramp-was-prompt-variance.md)
- **default flip** — the KV pool default follows `max_batch` instead of a hardcoded 8, and `serve` gains `--kv-tier` (the engine had `KvTier` wired but no CLI reach). Long context on this card is bounded by KV bytes, not the model: one 16-token block is 2.00 MiB of f32 K/V for the 16 full-attention layers, ~7.5 GiB of headroom remains after weights and states, so 16K fits at `--max-batch 2` and 32K needs `--max-batch 1`. The 48 gated-delta layers carry fixed-size state and cost nothing per token of context. An f16 pool would halve the block (32K → 4 GiB) but the pool dtype IS the attention kernel's ABI, so it needs an f16 `paged_attention_split` plus parity — not done. [serve-v100.md](docs/serve-v100.md)

- **phase exit** — the 27B serves end to end from the V100, first working completion on this card: two allocations nothing had ever summed. (1) `serve` sized its KV pool from `cfg.max_position_embeddings = 262144` — 131072 blocks × 2.10 MB = **275 GB** on a 32 GB card — because every bench script passes `num_blocks` explicitly and `serve` was the one caller that ever reached the default; new `--blocks` / `--max-ctx` (2048 / 4096 = 4.0 GiB, 8 rows at 4K). (2) Past that, `/health` answered ok at 30082 of 32510 MiB and the first token OOMed asking for 4.74 GiB: `materialize` had moved the bf16 248320×5120 embedding table to the card (2.37 GiB) and `embedding` then cast it to f32 **and cached that**, so one tensor held 7.11 GiB in two dtypes. sm70 cannot codegen a bf16 load (hence `Backend.io` f32) but loads f16 natively — `materialize` now casts to `embed_io` during the device move it was already doing and `embedding` widens in the gather, so the second copy is unrepresentable rather than merely absent. Accounted budget 24.31 GiB against torch's own 23.37 allocated (4% high, so nothing is unexplained); unchanged throughput (the table is off the critical path). Rehearsal cost four walls in front of the first token, each behind a ~5-minute weight load: KV sizing, `python3 -m tilerl` (no `__main__.py`), a privileged port, then the table. [wins/2026-09-02-embedding-table-in-two-dtypes.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-02-embedding-table-in-two-dtypes.md), [errors/2026-09-02-serve-never-sized-its-kv-pool.md](docs/experience/errors/2026-09-02-serve-never-sized-its-kv-pool.md), [serve-v100.md](docs/serve-v100.md)
- **default flip** — `serve` precaptures its decode graphs before printing its URL (`8 decode graphs in 19s`), and `--max-batch` drops 8 → 2 (one person, and the ceiling is also the grid to capture). First served request **0.9 → 3.23 → 13.62 tok/s** across no warmup, a token-generating warmup, and this; warmup cost **208 s → 19 s**, so precapture is 11× cheaper and 4.2× better on the request a person experiences. Serving cold reads 1088 ms/token and reaches 26.2 only by request six — every point between is a CUDA graph capture, one per (batch bucket × chain width), the width varying per tick because the draft's confidence truncates each chain, so no token count *guarantees* a width appears and generating more is a lottery with no floor. `Engine.precapture()` walks `graph_keys()` instead, and shares `_graph_bucket()` with `_run_decode_graph` so the two cannot disagree about which graphs exist. `_DecodeGraph` already carried the marker — "capture at engine build is the upgrade" — twice, in consecutive lines; both are gone. Cost a test on the way: the first version recomputed the bucket ladder inside the test file and its negative control PASSED, because **a test that reimplements its subject verifies only itself**. Gate `test_graph_keys_covers_what_a_decode_tick_keys_on`. Residual ramp 13.62 → 25.45 with all 8 graphs resident is the fp4 kernel JIT specializing per prefill shape — a different mechanism, unmeasured. [wins/2026-09-02-precapture-the-decode-graphs.md](docs/experience/wins/2026-09-02-precapture-the-decode-graphs.md)

Corrected — decode only, distinct prompts, equal counts — serve *improves* 1088 → 26.2 ms/token over six requests (`last/first = 0.02×`) against bench's 20.7, with free HBM flat at 2.81 GiB, so nothing leaks. Two instruments compounded it: a profiler timing from `submit` (2020 ms of prefill inside ms/token) and re-submitting one prompt, which is a prefix HIT — the opposite of what serve does. The GDN-snapshot suspect was refuted by a counter already in `/health` (`prefix_published` = 1 where the story needed 4; one 150 MiB clone is 0.003 ms/token against a 1088 ms token, three orders out). Daemon and HTTP cost nothing: a direct `step()` loop and `run()`+`take()` agree to 0.2% (64.9 vs 65.0 ms/token). Over-determined: feeding 5.53 back through four depths yields a clean verify staircase 36.58 / 49.87 / 49.87 / 68.46, rung ratios 1.363 and 1.373. 66.46 → 55.41 ms/tick, 50.3 → 60.3 tok/s, break-even 2.79 tok/forward against our 3.34 — and it needs a 5-layer/1.86B head where ours is 1 layer/456M, which agent-infer measured at 13% acceptance on Qwen3.8.
